Numerical analysis of first-order mean field games under displacement monotonicity
Alpár R. Mészáros, Yohance A. P. Osborne

Abstract
We introduce a particle method for the numerical approximation of time-dependent first-order Mean Field Games (MFGs) systems with non-separable, displacement monotone Hamiltonians and terminal costs, for arbitrary time-horizons and (possibly) singular initial player distributions in 𝒫 2 ​ ( ℝ d ) \mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . The numerical scheme is based on an implicit Euler discretization in time and sampling in space of the characteristic Hamiltonian/Pontryagin system associated with the continuous MFGs system. We prove convergence of the approximations of the player distribution in the L ∞ ​ ( 𝒲 2 ) L^{\infty}(\mathcal{W}_{2}) -metric and the approximations for the gradient of the value function along optimal trajectories in the L ∞ ​ ( L 2 ) L^{\infty}{(L^{2})} -norm as the number of spatial samples tends to infinity jointly with the temporal time-step vanishing. The error bound that we establish for this convergence further implies rates of convergence of the scheme for a range of spatial sampling techniques. Provided that the Lagrangian and terminal costs are additionally locally Lipschitz continuous, we also establish an asymptotic error bound in the L ∞ ​ ( L 1 ) L^{\infty}(L^{1}) -norm for the approximations of the value function along optimal trajectories. This is the first work in the literature on rigorous numerical approximation and analysis of first-order MFG systems that handles non-separable Hamiltonians and potentially singular initial agent distributions for arbitrary long time horizons. We illustrate the performance of the scheme in numerical experiments for a range of initial agent distributions, time horizons and space dimensions.



Introduction

Mean Field Games (MFGs) model Nash equilibria in (stochastic) differential games that involve a continuum of players. MFGs were initiated independently by Lasry–Lions in
[
58
,
59
,
60
]
in the mathematics literature and by Huang–Caines–Malhamé in
[
54
]
in the engineering community. MFGs models are often described by a nonlinear system of PDEs in which a Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equation — for the value function of a representative agent — is coupled with a Kolmogorov–Fokker–Planck (KFP) equation that describes the evolution of the players’ distribution. For an introduction to the theory, numerical analysis, and applications of MFGs, we refer the reader to
[
4
,
47
]
.

A prototypical first-order MFGs PDE system takes the form

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\partial_{t}u+H(x,-D_{x}u,\rho)=0,&\text{ in }(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\[3.0pt]
\partial_{t}\rho+D_{x}\cdot\left(\rho{D_{p}H}(x,-D_{x}u,\rho)\right)=0,&\text{ in }(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\[3.0pt]
u(T,x)=g(x,\rho(T))\quad\text{and}\quad\rho(0,\cdot)=\rho_{0},&\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

where
T>0
is a fixed time-horizon, the first equation being the HJB equation for the value function
u
of a typical agent and the second equation is the continuity equation (the ‘first order version’ of the KFP equation) for the unknown
(\rho_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
, a flow of probability measures describing the time evolution of the player population distribution. This system is equipped with an initial player distribution
\rho_{0}
. The forward-backward PDE system is nonlinearly coupled through both the Hamiltonian
H:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
and the terminal cost
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
, associated with the underlying optimal control problem faced by the generic player.

We would like to emphasise already at this point that Hamiltonian
H
has in general a so-called
non-separable
structure: the generalised momentum variable
p
is not supposed to be additively separated from the the distribution
\rho
. More precisely, we do not suppose the existence of maps
H_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
such that the additive decomposition

H(x,p,\mu)=H_{0}(x,p)-F(x,\mu),\quad\forall(x,p,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(1.2)

holds. Here we let
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the metric space of Borel probability measures supported on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with finite second moments, equipped with the
\mathcal{W}_{2}
-Wasserstein distance (see Section
2
below). Non-separable MFGs systems appear naturally in applications, such as in macroeconomics
[
1
]
or pedestrian dynamics
[
48
]
for instance.

Brief literature review relevant to our work.

Analysis of MFGs systems.
The existence and uniqueness of solutions to MFGs systems attracted increased attention in the past two decades. When establishing existence of solutions, different techniques have been applied, depending on the presence of regularising effect coming from either non-degenerate parabolicity or regularisation effects of the coupling functions
H
and
g
with respect to the measure component. When non-degenerate noise is present there are by now well-paved PDE tools establishing existence of smooth or weak solutions both in the local or nonlocal frameworks. We refer to
[
4
]
(and more particularly to
[
26
]
) for a detailed summary (as of around 2019 or so) of the various developments on these approaches, and to
[
46
]
for a regularity theory of classical solutions. Existence for first oder models, or for the ones which are degenerate parabolic, is much more intricate to study. Even for regularising couplings, in general one needs to assume further conditions, as regularity/summability/compact support of the initial measure and separability of the Hamiltonian in the sense of (
1.2
) (see for instance
[
26
, Section 1.3.4]
); in the case of local couplings weak solutions can be produced using variational techniques (cf.
[
27
,
24
,
25
,
81
,
52
]
). Remarkably, in the case of strong monotonicity properties such weak solutions can be proved to be more regular (cf.
[
49
,
52
]
) or even smooth (using a joint ‘elliptic structure’ in time-space, see
[
68
]
).

When it comes to the uniqueness and stability of solutions, usually more structural assumptions need to be imposed on the data
H
and
g
. These typically boil down to suitable
monotonicity conditions
. The most well-known regimes in this context are the so-called
Lasry–Lions (LL) monotonicity
and the
displacement (D) monotonicity
(for other possible monotonicity conditions and for their comparisons we refer to
[
50
]
). While for regularising couplings LL-monotonicity is known to be developed only in the case of separable Hamiltonians (
1.2
), in the case of local couplings this has a suitable analog (see for instance
[
6
]
). D-monotonicity is known to be developed only in the case of non-local Hamiltonians, and in general by default this does not need to assume any separability on
H
(cf.
[
69
,
43
]
), and gives not only uniqueness, but also existence and stability in a unified manner for first and second order models with general initial measures (cf.
[
44
,
13
]
). All this previous discussion is on results which are
global in time
.

Without monotonicity conditions well-posedness of MFGs systems involving general non-separable Hamiltonians from the point of view of classical solutions can be established at the price of suitable
smallness conditions
either on the time horizon, the size of the Hamiltonian, the initial measure or the combination of these. For a non-exhaustive list of works in these directions we refer to
[
10
,
9
,
8
]
.

Numerical analysis of MFG systems.
The analysis of numerical methods for MFGs systems with separable Hamiltonians (i.e. where the decomposition (
1.2
) holds) is an active area of research. Early works on the convergence analysis of monotone finite difference methods appeared in
[
3
,
2
,
5
]
, with more recent contributions concerning the convergence of policy iteration
[
21
,
22
]
and fictitious play schemes
[
55
,
85
]
, as well as the derivation of rates of convergence
[
18
]
. The convergence of semi-Lagrangian methods in one space dimension was shown in
[
31
,
32
]
for the first-order and degenerate second-order systems, respectively. This resulted in further works, such as the extension of the convergence analysis of
[
31
]
to arbitrary space dimension in
[
30
]
(see also
[
53
]
), the convergence of a semi-Lagrangian scheme for a non-degenerate second-order system in
[
33
]
, and the approximation of monotone operator formulations of first-order systems in
[
28
]
. In addition, a tensor-train-based high-order semi-Lagrangian policy iteration scheme was introduced in
[
29
]
for separable MFG systems with high-dimensional state space. Other numerical methods have also been studied, such as augmented Lagrangian methods
[
14
,
11
,
42
]
, kernel methods
[
63
,
70
,
7
]
, and approaches based on neural networks
[
62
]
.

The analysis and numerical approximation of MFGs systems with (possibly) nondifferentiable, but separable, Hamiltonians was recently initiated in
[
72
,
73
,
74
]
, where in particular convergence of a class of monotone stabilized finite element methods was shown. Since then, finite element error bounds for such schemes have appeared, such as
a priori
quasi-optimal error bounds in
[
75
]
and
a posteriori
error bounds in
[
71
,
84
]
, each addressing the case of systems with
C^{1,1}
Hamiltonians, as well as rates of convergence for MFGs systems with merely Lipschitz, nondifferentiable, separable Hamiltonians in
[
76
]
. An alternative approach to error analysis based on Brezzi–Rappaz–Raviart approximation theory was introduced in
[
15
]
for the finite element approximation of solutions to MFGs systems with
C^{2}
-separable Hamiltonians that admit multiple solutions; see also
[
16
]
for an extension of this approach to the case of
C^{1,1}
-separable Hamiltonians based on the notion of metric-regular mappings.

The analysis of numerical schemes for second-order MFG systems in the case of non-separable Hamiltonians seems to have been considered only in
[
61
,
12
,
23
]
. In particular, a policy iteration method was analyzed in
[
61
]
for a second-order non-separable system which was then applied to a finite difference discretization of the system to generate simulations. In
[
12
]
an algorithm was studied for a second-order non-separable system, wherein the HJB and KFP equations are each approximated by a deep neural network within an iteration procedure for the entire system. The quadratic convergence of a Newton iteration scheme in infinite-dimensional function spaces was shown in
[
23
]
for a class of second-order non-separable MFG systems. Turning to the case of systems that are both first-order and non-separable, to the best of our knowledge, only numerical algorithms, without their analysis, have been introduced for solving a very particular MFGs system in the work
[
67
]
. Therein, an iterative algorithm based on approximating the forward-backward Pontryagin system for the player trajectories was proposed, without analysis, to obtain simulations of minimal-time MFG models with initial distributions being Dirac measures supported on a single point. It appears therefore that the analysis of numerical methods for first-order, non-separable MFG systems has been largely unexplored until our current work.

Our contributions.

In this paper, we focus on the numerical approximation of the MFG system (
1.1
) that is both first-order and non-separable. As our main contribution, we develop a
particle-based
numerical method for the system (
1.1
) where the Hamiltonian
H
and the terminal cost
g
are in particular displacement monotone, and the initial player distribution
\rho_{0}
is allowed to be an arbitrary measure
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For this, we fix a reference probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
with
\mathbb{P}
being non-atomic (to ensure than any
\rho\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be realised as the law of a random variable
X\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
). Without loss of generality,
\Omega
can be taken to be a compact set of a Euclidean space, with unit Lebesgue measure and
\mathbb{P}
can be chosen to be the restriction of the Lebesgue measure to
\Omega
.

Suppose that
(\rho(s))_{s\in[0,T]}
is the MFG equilibrium flow of probability measures and let
X_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a given initial random variable distributed according to
\rho_{0}
. The starting point for the method is the deterministic forward-backward Hamiltonian type system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}X(t)&=X_{0}+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}D_{p}H\left(X(s),Y(s),\rho(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s,\quad\text{in }C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})),\\
Y(t)&\displaystyle=-D_{x}g\left(X(T),\rho(T)\right)+\int_{t}^{T}D_{x}H\left(X(s),Y(s),\rho(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s,\quad\text{in }C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})),\end{array}\right.

(1.3)

together with the fixed point condition that the law of
X(s)
is precisely
\rho_{s}
, i.e.

X(s)_{\#}\mathbb{P}=\rho_{s},\ \ \forall s\in[0,T].

(1.4)

It was shown in
[
69
]
that under suitable regularity and D-monotonicity assumptions on
H,g
, this Hamiltonian system has a unique global in time solution pair
(X,Y)
(in particular
X
satisfes (
1.4
)) which is in a one-to-one correspondence with the unique solution
(u,\rho)
to the MFG system (
1.1
). In formal terms, this relationship is as follows: the momentum flow
Y\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
satisfies
Y(t)=-D_{x}u(t,X(t))
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
t\in[0,T]
, which is the flow of the negative of the value function’s gradient
-D_{x}u
along the players’ characteristic path
X\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, that uniquely satisfies the forward equation of (
1.3
) with
\rho(t)=(X(t))_{\#}\mathbb{P}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
t\in[0,T]
(see Section
2
for further details). While in this system there is no randomness coming from idiosyncratic or common noises, the role of the probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
and the Lebesgue space
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the corresponding
L^{2}
-random variables is to model the dependence on the initial measure
\rho_{0}
and its approximations in a robust way, and give a functional analytic setting, suitable for our analysis. We will benefit from this setting throughout the analysis later.

In view of this connection between
(u,\rho)
and
(X,Y)
, we introduce a discretisation of the continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
) as a numerical scheme to approximate both
\rho
and
D_{x}u(t,X)
. More precisely, the numerical scheme is a discrete Hamiltonian system whose solutions
\{X^{k,N}\}_{k,N}
,
\{Y^{k,N}\}_{k,N}
are piecewise constant in time and defined everywhere in
[0,T]
, with constant step-size
\tau_{k}>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, whose values are simple random variables in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
that assume
N
distinct values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
. The formulation of the numerical method, which we present in Section
3
, is structurally consistent with the continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
) and employs merely deterministic empirical approximations of
\rho_{0}
, thereby allowing for (possibly) purely singular initial player distributions for the MFG model problem. Here
k\in\mathbb{N}
is the index of the time step
\tau_{k}
, while
N\in\mathbb{N}
refers to the discretization level of the initial measure
\rho_{0}
.

The main guiding principle of our analysis is the following observation: first, under D-monotonicity the HJB equation in (
1.1
) has a unique classical solution such that
D_{x}u(t,\cdot)\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
; second, this combined with the deterministic nature of the problem implies that if
\rho_{0}
is a finitely supported measure,
t\mapsto\rho_{t}
will remain a finitely supported measure flow throughout
t\in[0,T]
, and collisions of the Dirac masses/crossing of characteristics cannot occur. This precisely means that if
X_{0}
is taken to be a simple random variable (i.e. with finite range), this will be maintained for the solution
(X_{t},Y_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
of the Hamiltonian system as well, throughout the time evolution. Our numerical approach is hence based on these crucial facts.

Our main results concerning well-posedness for the scheme are given in Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
4.2
where we prove, respectively, the existence and uniqueness of the approximations
\{(X^{k,N},Y^{k,N})\}_{{k,N}}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. At the heart of the analysis is a continuous dependence bound that we prove in Theorem
5.1
for discrete Hamiltonian systems under affine perturbations. Therein, we show the key property that the discrete Hamiltonian system remarkably
inherits
the D-monotonicity from the D-monotonicity property satisfied by the continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
), which ultimately implies
uniqueness and stability
of our discrete approximations. As such, we have developed a
D-monotone
numerical scheme, in the sense of displacement monotonicity of the nonlinear couplings, that applies to the MFG system (
1.1
) even if the initial player distribution is not absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure or does not have compact support. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first use of D-monotonicity in developing a well-posed numerical method for MFGs systems.

The next main result concerns the strong convergence of the numerical scheme in the joint limit as
k,N\to\infty
. In fact, we prove a stronger result in Theorem
4.3
, where we establish the asymptotic error bound

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\rho(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(-D_{x}u(\cdot,X)-Y^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(X-X^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\left(\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}+\tau_{k}\right)

(1.5)

for all
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
k
sufficiently large, where the constant
C>0
depends only on parameters associated with the model data of the continuous problem. Here,
\rho^{k,N}(t)\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the law of
X^{k,N}(t)
for each
t\in[0,T]
and
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
. The approximations
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
for
X_{0}
are a given sequence of simple random variables that are distributed according to a corresponding sequence of deterministic finitely supported measures
\{\rho_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
for
\rho_{0}
, where
\rho_{0}^{N}
is the law of
X_{0}^{N}
for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges strongly to
X_{0}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Crucially, we show that the above error bound (
1
) is a consequence of the continuous dependence bound in Theorem
5.1
for discrete Hamiltonian systems and the
W^{1,\infty}
-in-time regularity of the solution
(X,Y)
to the continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
). If both the terminal cost
g
and the Lagrangian
L(x,v,\mu)\coloneqq\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\{p\cdot v-H(x,p,\mu)\}
are additionally locally Lipschitz continuous, then we further prove in Theorem
4.4
that the discrete value function

\displaystyle u^{k,N}(t)\coloneqq\int_{t}^{T}L(X^{k,N}(s),{D_{p}H(X^{k,N}(s),Y^{k,N}(s),\rho^{k,N}(s))},\rho^{k,N}(s))\mathrm{d}s+g(X^{k,N}(T),\rho^{k,N}(T)),\ \forall t\in[0,T],

(1.6)

constructed from the approximations
\{(X^{k,N},Y^{k,N},\rho^{k,N})\}_{k,N}
, satisfies the error bound

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(u(\cdot,X)-u^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}+\tau_{k}

for all
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
both sufficiently large.

We then obtain two corollaries on deriving explicit rates of convergence for the scheme according to the choice of the piecewise constant spatial approximations
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
. For example, if
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is generated through optimal transport maps associated with the semi-discrete optimal transport problem for
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho_{0},\rho_{0}^{N})
, for a suitable class of empirical measures
\{\rho_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, then Corollary
4.5
gives the error bound

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\rho(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(-D_{x}u(\cdot,X)-Y^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(X-X^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\begin{cases}\max\{N^{-1/d},N^{-1/2+1/q}\}+\tau_{k},&\text{if }d\neq\frac{2q}{q-2},\\
N^{-1/d}\log(1+N)^{1/d}+\tau_{k},&\text{if }d=\frac{2q}{q-2},\end{cases}

(1.7)

for all
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k
sufficiently large, provided
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
q>2
, is absolutely continuous with respect to the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure. Alternatively, if
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is obtained through simple vector quantization of
X_{0}
, Corollary
4.7
gives the error bound

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\rho(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(-D_{x}u(\cdot,X)-Y^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(X-X^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C\left(N^{-1/d}+\tau_{k}\right),

(1.8)

for all
N,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k
sufficiently large, provided that
\rho_{0}
has a non-trivial absolutely continuous part and
X_{0}\in L^{2+\delta}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
\delta>0
, or equivalently
\rho_{0}
has finite
(2+\delta)
-moment, for some
\delta>0
. In the case where
L
and
g
are additionally locally Lipschitz continuous, we deduce corresponding error bounds in
L^{\infty}(L^{1})
-norm for the discrete value function approximation
u^{k,N}
that inherit the above asymptotic convergence rates.

We conclude this work with numerical experiments that illustrate the performance of our numerical scheme. In particular, in Section
8
we propose two iterative algorithms that approximate the nonlinear discrete forward-backward Hamiltonian system satisfied by
(X^{k,N},Y^{k,N})
for given
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
. The first method, Algorithm
A
, is based on a standard Picard iteration globally in time. Motivated by the fact that the convergence of such Picard iterations requires that the time horizon
T
is sufficiently small, we propose an alternative approach, namely Algorithm
B
, to approximate the discrete problem for longer time horizons. Our approach is inspired by the work of Chassagneux–Crisan–Delarue
[
34
]
in which they employ local-in-time Picard iterations to obtain pointwise approximations of solutions to a class of master equations posed on Wasserstein space, where the approximations are shown to remain stable independently of the size of the time horizon. In Algorithm
B
, we generate local-in-time Picard iterations within a global-in-time Picard fixed-point scheme to approximate the nonlinear discrete Hamiltonian system. Here we find yet another application of the general continuous dependence bound of Theorem
5.1
, which we use to establish an
a posteriori
error bound for the iteration scheme given by Algorithm
B
, for the simplified scenario where each of the local-in-time Picard iterations return the exact discrete solution within one iteration.

The numerical experiments involve examples of MFG systems where we use reference solutions or have access to an exact solution, and where the initial agent distribution has compact support or not. In particular, the first two experiments showcase that both Algorithm
A
and Algorithm
B
can approximate the system equally well (in the sense of having nearly identical relative errors) when
T
is not large (such as
T=1
), but that Algorithm
B
can be used to solve the discrete system when
T
assumes larger values such as
T\in\{2,4,8,16,32\}
while preserving theoretically predicted asymptotic convergence rates independently of
T
. These experiments hence showcase robustness of our numerical method for longer time horizons. For the final numerical experiment we illustrate the space-dimension dependence of the asymptotic rate of convergence for the numerical scheme when applied to an MFG with absolutely continuous initial measure supported everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
. By initializing the numerical scheme using vector quantizers for
X_{0}
, in this test we observe an asymptotic convergence rate of order
1/d
, which is consistent with the conclusion of Corollary
4.7
. To the best of our knowledge, our method is the first to approximate first-order, non-separable MFG systems with theoretically predicted rates of convergence.

Outline.

The rest of the manuscript is structured as follows. We set basic notation and model assumptions for the MFG system (
1.1
) in Section
2
. This is followed by the formulation of the continuous Hamiltonian system in Section
2
, where in particular we discuss its connection with the MFG system (
1.1
) in terms of continuous dependence with respect to discrete approximations of the initial data. We then dedicate Section
3
to introducing notation for a suite of vector spaces consisting of Banach space-valued maps that are piecewise constant in time. We employ such vector spaces in Section
3
where we formulate our discrete numerical scheme for the continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
). In Section
4
, we present the main results on well-posedness and convergence, as well as corollaries on rates of convergence, for the numerical scheme. The proof of the key continuous dependence bound for discrete Hamiltonian systems under affine perturbations is the subject of Section
5
. We then employ this bound to prove the existence of discrete approximations in Section
6
and the convergence of such approximations in Section
7
. Algorithms for solving the numerical scheme are given in Section
8
, which is then followed by the results of three numerical experiments therein. We include proofs of some auxiliary results in Appendix
A
.

Setting and Continuous Problem

Basic notation.

Given
q\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{P}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the set of Borel probability measures supported on Boreal measurable subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite
q
-moment, and for
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we denote its
q
-moment by
M_{q}(\mu)\coloneqq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{q}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}
. Let
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
denote a non-atomic reference probability space. Without loss of generality, it is convenient for us to choose
\Omega\coloneqq[0,1]^{d}
to be the unit cube,
\mathbb{F}
the
\sigma
-algebra of Lebesgue measurable subsets of
\Omega
and
\mathbb{P}
the Lebesgue measure restricted to
\Omega
. For
q\in[1,\infty)
, we denote by
L^{q}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variables
X
on
\Omega
that are Lebesgue measurable and
\int_{\Omega}|X(\omega)|^{q}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)<\infty
. We equip
L^{q}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the standard norm denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{L^{q}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. For the special case when
q=2
, we equip
{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
with the standard inner product that is induced by expectation:

\mathbb{E}[V\cdot W]\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}V\cdot W\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}
for
V,W\in{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, with the corresponding norm denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. As such,
{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
is a Hilbert space. Given
q\geq 1
,
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\xi\in L^{q}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we say that
\mu
is the law of
\xi
if
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\coloneqq\xi_{\#}\mathbb{P}=\mu
. Here, the push-forward notation
\xi_{\#}\mathbb{P}=\mu
means
\mu(A)=\mathbb{P}(\xi^{-1}(A))
for any Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For
1\leq q<\infty
, we equip
\mathcal{P}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the
\mathcal{W}_{q}
-Wasserstein metric, which for all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined as

\mathcal{W}_{q}(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq\inf\left\{\left(\mathbb{E}[|\xi-\eta|^{q}]\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}:\ \ \xi,\eta\in L^{q}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ {\rm with}\ \mu=\mathcal{L}_{\xi},\nu=\mathcal{L}_{\eta}\right\}.

(2.1)

Vector spaces generated by simple random variables.

An
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued simple random variable
\xi
is a function in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that, for some finite
M\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist sets of (nontrivial) weights
\mathcal{A}\coloneq\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}\subset(0,1]
, points
\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and subsets
\mathcal{O}\coloneq\{\Omega_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}\subset\mathbb{F}
such that
\xi\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{M}x_{j}\chi_{\Omega_{j}}
where
\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{j}=1
,
\cup_{j=1}^{M}\overline{\Omega_{j}}=\Omega
,
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{j})=\alpha_{j}
for
j\in\{1,\cdots,{M}\}
, and
\Omega_{j}\cap\Omega_{i}=\emptyset
if
i\neq j
in
\{1,\cdots,{M}\}
. In this case, we have that
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}=\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{j}\delta_{x_{j}}
. Furthermore, it is clear that
\xi
lives in the finite-dimensional vector space of all
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued simple random variables on
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the form
W=\sum_{j=1}^{M}w_{j}\chi_{\Omega_{j}}
such that
\{w_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We let
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O})
denote this
{M}
-dimensional vector space generated by
\xi
which consists of all
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued simple random variables supported on the subsets
\mathcal{O}
with corresponding weights
\mathcal{A}
. Notice that for each
V,W\in\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O})
the scalar product
V\cdot W
yields an
\mathbb{R}
-valued simple random variable on
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
where
V\cdot W=\sum_{i=1}^{M}v_{i}\cdot w_{i}\chi_{\Omega_{i}}

and taking expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}
gives
\mathbb{E}\left[V\cdot W\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}v_{i}\cdot w_{i}
. As such, the space
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O})
generated by
\xi
, when equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, is a finite dimensional subspace of
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Standing assumptions.

We let
H:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
denote the Hamiltonian of the MFG system (
1.1
). We assume that
H
is continuous, where the continuity is with respect to
\mathcal{W}_{1}
in the measure variable, and which is locally uniform with respect to the
(x,p)
variable. Furthermore,
H

satisfies

\bullet\ \ H(\cdot,\cdot,\mu)\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \forall\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.2)

\hskip 15.0pt\bullet\ \ D_{p}H,D_{x}H\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d});\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.3)

where the Lipschitz continuity is understood with respect to
\mathcal{W}_{1}
in the measure variable. Note that this Lipschitz continuity then also holds with respect to the weaker
\mathcal{W}_{q}
metric in the measure variable for any
q\in[1,\infty)
.

It then follows that there exist constants
C_{D_{p}H}>0
and
C_{D_{x}H}>0
such that

\displaystyle|D_{p}H(x,p,\mu)|\leq C_{D_{p}H}\left(|x|+|p|+{\mathcal{W}_{q}}(\mu,\delta_{0})+1\right)

(2.4a)

\displaystyle|D_{x}H(x,p,\mu)|\leq C_{D_{x}H}\left(|x|+|p|+{\mathcal{W}_{q}}(\mu,\delta_{0})+1\right)

(2.4b)

for all
(x,p,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all
q\in[1,\infty)
. We also assume that

\bullet\ \ \mathbb{R}^{d}\ni p\mapsto H(x,p,\mu)\text{ is strongly convex\ }\text{uniformly in }(x,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.5)

i.e. there exists
c_{0}>0
such that

D_{pp}^{2}H\geq c_{0}I_{d}\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.6)

We assume that

|D_{p}H(0,p,\mu)|\leq C(p)\quad\forall(p,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.7)

for some positive function
\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni p\mapsto C(p)>0
that is independent of
\mu
.

We further assume that the Hamiltonian
H
is
displacement monotone
in the sense that

\begin{split}&\bullet\ \ \mathbb{E}\Bigl[\left(D_{p}H(X_{1},Y_{1},\mathcal{L}_{X_{1}})-D_{p}H(X_{2},Y_{2},\mathcal{L}_{X_{2}})\right)\cdot(Y_{1}-Y_{2})\\
&\qquad\qquad-\left(D_{x}H(X_{1},Y_{1},\mathcal{L}_{X_{1}})-D_{x}H(X_{2},Y_{2},\mathcal{L}_{X_{2}})\right)\cdot(X_{1}-X_{2})\Bigr]\geq 0\end{split}

(2.8)

for any
X_{i},Y_{i}\in{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
.

The terminal cost
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
of the MFG system (
1.1
) is assumed to be continuous, where the continuity is with respect to
\mathcal{W}_{1}
in the measure variable. Furthermore,
g
is supposed to satisfy

\hskip 5.0pt\bullet\ \ g(\cdot,\mu)\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \forall\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.9)

\bullet\ \ D_{x}g\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d});\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.10)

where the Lipschitz continuity is with respect to
\mathcal{W}_{1}
in the measure variable. We note then that this Lipschitz continuity also holds with respect to the weaker
\mathcal{W}_{q}
metric in the measure variable for any
q\in[1,\infty)
. As a consequence,
there exists a constant
C_{D_{x}g}>0
such that

|D_{x}g(x,\mu)|\leq C_{D_{x}g}\left(|x|+\mathcal{W}_{q}(\mu,\delta_{0})+1\right),\quad\forall(x,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.11)

for all
q\in[1,\infty)
. We also assume that
g
satisfies the
displacement monotonicity
condition, in the sense that

\bullet\ \ \mathbb{E}\left[\left(D_{x}g({X}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{X_{1}})-D_{x}g({X}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{X_{2}})\right)\cdot({X}_{1}-{X}_{2})\right]\geq 0

(2.12)

for any
{X}_{1},X_{2}\in{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. It follows that
\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni x\mapsto g(x,\mu)
is convex, uniformly in
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(cf.
[
69
]
).

Finally, let
L:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be the Lagrangian function associated to
H
, i.e.

L(x,v,\mu)\coloneqq\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{p\cdot v-H(x,p,\mu)\right\},

for
all
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. It follows from standard convex analysis that the regularity (
2.2
) and uniform Lipschitz continuity of
D_{p}H
and
D_{x}H
via (
2.3
), together with strong convexity of
H
with respect to
p
via (
2.5
) and (
2.6
), altogether imply

\bullet\ \ L(\cdot,\cdot,\mu)\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \forall\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.13)

\hskip 15.0pt\bullet\ \ D_{v}L,D_{x}L\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d});\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.14)

where the Lipschitz continuity is understood with respect to
\mathcal{W}_{1}
in the measure variable, and which hence also holds with respect
\mathcal{W}_{q}
for any
q\in[1,\infty)
. We note that the uniform Lipschitz continuity of
D_{x}L
and
D_{v}L
imply that there exist constants
C_{D_{v}L}>0
and
C_{D_{x}L}>0
such that

\displaystyle|D_{v}L(x,v,\mu)|\leq C_{D_{v}L}\left(|x|+|v|+{\mathcal{W}_{q}}(\mu,\delta_{0})+1\right)

(2.15a)

\displaystyle|D_{x}L(x,v,\mu)|\leq C_{D_{x}L}\left(|x|+|v|+{\mathcal{W}_{q}}(\mu,\delta_{0})+1\right)

(2.15b)

for all
(x,v,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all
q\in[1,\infty)
.
Throughout, we assume that

L(x,v,\mu)\geq\theta_{1}(|v|)-\theta_{2}(\mu)(|x|+1),\quad g(x,\mu)\geq-\theta_{2}(\mu),\quad\forall(x,q,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(2.16)

for some superlinear function
\theta_{1}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
and some function
\theta_{2}:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to[0,\infty)
which is bounded in
\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):M_{2}(\mu)\leq R\}
, for any
R>0
.

Throughout this paper, the initial agent distribution is taken to be a general measure
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, without any further assumptions, unless specified in particular examples.

For notational purposes, for real numbers
a
and
b
, we write
a\lesssim b
if there exists a constant
C
such that
a\leq Cb
where
C>0
(naturally, independent of
a
and
b
) depends only on the problem data
c_{0}
,
T
,
{\rm Lip}(D_{p}H)
,
{\rm Lip}(D_{x}H)
,
{\rm Lip}(D_{v}L)
,
{\rm Lip}(D_{x}L)
,
{\rm Lip}(D_{x}g)
, but is otherwise independent of all temporal and spatial discretisation parameters. We write
a\eqsim b
if
a\lesssim b
and
b\lesssim a
.

Example 2.1
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}>0
be constants (whose values will be specified). Let
\psi,\nu\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
both be non-negative functions. Let
\phi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\upsilon\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that
p\mapsto\phi(x,p)
is convex, uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
x\mapsto\phi(x,p)
is concave, uniformly in
p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\upsilon
is convex, and each of the derivatives
D_{p}\phi
,
D_{x}\phi
, and
D_{x}\upsilon
are uniformly bounded and Lipschitz continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, respectively. Consider the Hamiltonian
H:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
given by

H(x,p,\mu)\coloneqq\frac{C_{1}}{2}\left(|p|^{2}-|x|^{2}\right)+\phi(x,p)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(z)d\mu(z)\quad\forall(x,p,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(2.17)

and the terminal cost
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
of the form

g(x,\mu)\coloneqq\frac{C_{2}}{2}|x|^{2}+\upsilon(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nu(z)d\mu(z)\quad\forall(x,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.18)

By a direct calculation, one can show that the above
H
satisfies the regularity assumptions (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.5
), (
2.6
), (
2.7
) and displacement monotonicity condition (
2.8
) for
C_{1}
satisfying

C_{1}\geq 2\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}({\rm Lip}(D_{p}\phi)+{\rm Lip}(D_{x}\phi))+2{\rm Lip}(\psi)(\|D_{x}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|D_{p}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})}).

Similarly, one can show that
g
satisfies the regularity assumptions (
2.9
), (
2.10
), and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.12
) for
C_{2}\geq{\rm Lip}(D_{x}\upsilon)\|\nu\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+{\rm Lip}(\nu)\|D_{x}\upsilon\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. It can be shown that the constants
C_{1},C_{2}
can be taken to be sufficiently large so that, in addition, the growth conditions (
2.16
) on the Lagrangian
L
and
g
hold with
\theta_{1}(r)\coloneqq\frac{r^{2}}{2C_{1}}
,
r\geq 0
, and
\theta_{2}\coloneqq C_{*}
on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with constant
C_{*}
sufficiently large that depends only on
\|D_{p}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,
\|D_{x}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,
\|D_{x}\upsilon\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})}
,
\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
, and
\|\nu\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
.

The Continuous problem: existence and uniqueness of solutions.

Let
X_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a random variable such that
\rho_{0}=\mathcal{L}_{X_{0}}
. We consider solutions to the Hamiltonian system (
1.3
) in the following sense:
find
(X,Y,\rho)\in C^{1}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times C^{1}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}X(t)&\displaystyle=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}D_{p}H\left(X(s),Y(s),\rho(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s,&\text{in }[0,T]\\[3.0pt]
Y(t)&=\displaystyle-D_{x}g\left(X(T),\rho(T)\right)+\int_{t}^{T}D_{x}H\left(X(s),Y(s),\rho(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s,&\text{in }[0,T]\\[3.0pt]
\rho(t)&=X(t)_{\#}\mathbb{P}&\text{in }[0,T].\end{array}\right.

(2.19)

Remark 2.1
.

For the particular case when
\rho_{0}
is a deterministic measure of finite support of the form
\rho_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{\alpha_{i}}\delta_{\tilde{x}_{i}}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for some finite set of points
\{\tilde{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and weights
\{\tilde{\alpha}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\subset(0,1]
,
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\tilde{\alpha}_{j}=1,

m\in\mathbb{N}
, we assume in the Hamiltonian system (
1.3
) that
X_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i,m}\chi_{\Omega_{i,m}}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
where
x_{i,m}\coloneqq\tilde{x}_{i}
and
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{i,m})=\alpha_{i,m}
with
\alpha_{i,m}\coloneqq\tilde{\alpha}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
.

We recall that
it is known from
[
69
, Theorem 4.1 & Theorem 4.5]
that, for each initial random variable
X_{0}
distributed according to
\rho_{0}
, the Hamiltonian system (
2.19
) admits a unique solution
(X,Y,\rho)
. This solution is determined by the unique pair
(u,\rho)
which satisfies the first-order mean field game PDE system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\partial_{t}u+H(x,-D_{x}u,\rho)=0&\text{ in }(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\[3.0pt]
\partial_{t}\rho+D_{x}\cdot\left(\rho{D_{p}H}(x,-D_{x}u,\rho)\right)=0&\text{ in }(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\[3.0pt]
u(T,x)=g(x,\rho(T))\quad\text{and}\quad\rho(0,x)=\rho_{0},&\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{array}\right.

(2.20)

in the sense that
u
is the unique classical solution (of class
C^{1,1}_{\rm loc}
) of the Hamilton–Jacobi equation and
\rho
is the unique distributional solution of the continuity equation. The existence and uniqueness of such a pair
(u,\rho)
is ensured by
[
69
, Corollary 4.6]
under the regularity and growth assumptions (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.5
), (
2.6
), (
2.7
), (
2.9
), (
2.10
), (
2.16
) and the displacement monotonicity conditions (
2.8
), (
2.12
) on
H
and
g
.

[
69
, Theorem 4.1]
then shows that

Y(t)\coloneqq-D_{x}u(t,X(t))\quad\text{in }[0,T],

(2.21)

and that
X
is the unique solution of the forward equation

X(t)=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}D_{p}H\left(X(s),-D_{x}u(s,X(s)),\rho(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\text{in }[0,T],

(2.22)

with
\rho(t)=X(t)_{\#}\mathbb{P}
,
t\in[0,T]
. In particular
X,Y\in C^{1}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\rho
is an element in the space
C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Consequently, the value function evaluated along the optimal trajectory
(X(t),{Y(t)})_{t\in[0,T]}
has the representation

u(t,X(t))=\int_{t}^{T}L(X(s),{D_{p}H(X(s),Y(s),\rho(s))},\rho(s))\mathrm{d}s+g(X(T),\rho(T))\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

(2.23)

Continuous dependence of solutions on discrete initial conditions.

Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\rho_{0}^{N}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha_{j,N}\delta_{x_{j,N}}
denote a deterministic measure approximation of
\rho_{0}
with finite support in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
where
\text{card}(\text{spt}(\rho_{0}^{N}))=N
and
\mathcal{A}_{N}\coloneqq\{\alpha_{j,N}\}_{j=1}^{N}\subset(0,1]
with
\sum_{j=1}^{N}\alpha_{j,N}=1
. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
X_{0}^{N}
denote an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued simple random variable distributed according to
\rho_{0}^{N}
with respect to the reference probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
of the form
X_{0}^{N}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}x_{j,N}\chi_{\Omega_{j,N}}
for some subsets
\mathcal{O}_{N}\coloneqq\{\Omega_{j,N}\}_{j=1}^{N}\subset\mathbb{F}
such that
\cup_{j=1}^{N}\overline{\Omega_{j,N}}=\Omega
,
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{j,N})=\alpha_{j,N}
for
j\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
, and
\Omega_{j,N}\cap\Omega_{i,N}=\emptyset
if
i\neq j
in
\{1,\cdots,N\}
. Therefore, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the simple random variable
X_{0}^{N}
generates the subspace
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
of
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\mathcal{A}_{N}=\{\alpha_{j,N}\}_{j=1}^{N}
and
\mathcal{O}_{N}=\{\Omega_{j,N}\}_{j=1}^{N}
. Throughout this work, we assume that the sequences
\{\rho_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, and hence the sequences of weights
\{\mathcal{A}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and subsets
\{\mathcal{O}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, are given and fixed, unless otherwise specified.

Applying once more
[
69
, Theorem 4.5]
, we have that for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a unique solution
(X^{N},Y^{N},\rho^{N})
to the continuous Hamiltonian system (
2.19
) with
X_{0}^{N}
as the initial random variable of the system, with the corresponding value function
u^{N}
satisfying

u^{N}(t,X^{N}(t))=\int_{t}^{T}L(X^{N}(s),{D_{p}H(X^{N}(s),Y^{N}(s),\rho^{N}(s))},\rho^{N}(s))\mathrm{d}s+g(X^{N}(T),\rho^{N}(T)),\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

(2.24)

The following result concerns the convergence of the sequence
\{(X^{N},Y^{N},\rho^{N})\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, whose proof is omitted since it is a direct application of the argument behind the continuous dependence result given in
[
69
, Theorem 4.5]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let the Hamiltonian
H
satisfy the regularity and growth assumptions (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.5
), (
2.6
), (
2.7
), (
2.16
) and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.8
). Let the terminal cost
g
satisfy the regularity and growth assumptions (
2.9
), (
2.10
), (
2.16
) and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.12
). Let
X_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
be given such that
\rho_{0}=\mathcal{L}_{X_{0}}
. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\rho_{0}^{N}
be a deterministic measure of finite support with
{\rm card}({\rm spt}(\rho_{0}^{N}))=N
and let
X_{0}^{N}
be a simple random variable distributed according to
\rho_{0}^{N}
. Let
(X,Y,\rho)\in C^{1}([0,T];{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})})\times C^{1}([0,T];{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})})\times C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
denote the triple that uniquely satisfies the system (
2.19
) with initial random variable
X_{0}
, and let
\{(X^{N},Y^{N},\rho^{N})\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{1}([0,T];{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})})\times C^{1}([0,T];{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})})\times C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
denote the sequence of unique solutions to the system (
2.19
) corresponding to the sequence of initial random variables
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then,

\begin{split}&\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|(-D_{x}u(\cdot,X)-Y^{N})(t)\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|(X-X^{N})(t)\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho(t),\rho^{N}(t))\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\lesssim\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}},\quad\forall N\in\mathbb{N}.\end{split}

(2.25)

In particular, if
X_{0}^{N}\to X_{0}
in
{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
as
N\to\infty
, then

\displaystyle X^{N}\to X\text{ and }Y^{N}\to-D_{x}u(\cdot,X){\rm\ in\ }C([0,T];{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})})

(2.26a)

\displaystyle\rho^{N}\to\rho=(X(t))_{\#}\mathbb{P}\quad{\rm\ in\ }C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),

(2.26b)

as
N\to\infty
.

We obtain a corollary for continuous dependence of the value function along the optimal trajectory provided that
L
(equivalently
H
) and
g
are additionally locally Lipschitz continuous in all variables.

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
u
denote the value function in the continuous MFG, represented along optimal trajectories in (
2.23
) and let
u^{N}
stand for the discretised value function defined in (
2.24
).
In addition to the hypotheses of Lemma
2.1
, suppose that for each
R>0
there exists a constant
C_{R}>0
such that

\displaystyle|L(x,v,\mu)-L(y,w,\nu)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\left(1+|x|+|y|+|v|+|w|+\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\delta_{0})+\mathcal{W}_{1}(\nu,\delta_{0})\right)(|x-y|+|v-w|+\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu))

(2.27a)

\displaystyle|g(x,\mu)-g(y,\nu)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\left(1+|x|+|y|+\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\delta_{0})+\mathcal{W}_{1}(\nu,\delta_{0})\right)(|x-y|+\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu))

(2.27b)

whenever
(x,v),(y,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy
|x-y|+|y-w|+\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)<R
. If
X_{0}^{N}\to X_{0}
in
{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
as
N\to\infty
, then

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|(u(\cdot,X)-u^{N}(\cdot,X^{N})(t)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\lesssim\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}

(2.28)

for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large.

This result is a direct consequence of the representation formulae (
2.23
) and (
2.24
), the standing assumptions on
H
and
g
, and the additional local Lipschitz hypotheses (
2.27a
), (
2.27b
). For completeness, we present its proof in Section
7
.

Let us emphasise that the additional assumptions on the local Lipschitz properties of
L
and
g
(in the measure variable) are not a consequence of the previously imposed regularity assumptions. We note that all the previous regularity assumptions in the measure variable are imposed on
D_{x}g
,
D_{x}H,D_{p}H
(or equivalently on
D_{x}L,D_{v}L
) and in particular no regularity or Lipschitz property was imposed on
L
and
g
in the measure variable.

We conclude this section with discussion of two constructions for the sequence of deterministic measures
\{\rho_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of finite support and corresponding random variables
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
that converge strongly to
X_{0}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Each example is also accompanied by rates of convergence that hold under sufficient conditions.

Example 2.2

(Semi-discrete optimal transport)
.

Given a measure
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it is well known that
\rho_{0}
can be approximated by sequences of empirical measures
\rho_{0}^{N}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
which are stochastic
[
17
,
40
,
41
]
or deterministic
[
35
,
79
,
82
]
. In particular, assuming that
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
q>2
,
[
82
, Theorem 1]
provides an algorithmic construction of a sequence of uniformly weighted deterministic empirical measures
\{\rho_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
that converge strongly to
\rho_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
N\to\infty
, with the error bounds

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho_{0},\rho_{0}^{N})\leq CM_{q}(\rho_{0})\times\begin{cases}\max\{N^{-1/d},N^{-1/2+1/q}\},&{\rm if\ }d\neq\frac{2q}{q-2},\\
N^{-1/d}\log(1+N)^{1/d},&{\rm if\ }d=\frac{2q}{q-2},\end{cases}\quad\forall N\in\mathbb{N}

(2.29)

and
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho_{0},\rho_{0}^{N})\leq CN^{-1/2+1/q}
for
N\in\mathbb{N}
if
q<\infty
and
d<\frac{2q}{q-2}
. The constant
C
in both of these bounds here depends only on
d
and
q
. Assuming further that
\rho_{0}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{P}
, Brenier’s Theorem
[
20
]
then ensures that, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an optimal transport map
\mathcal{T}^{N}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, defined everywhere with
{\rm card}({\rm Ran}(T^{N}))=N
, which satisfies
\mathcal{T}^{N}(X_{0})\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\|X_{0}-\mathcal{T}^{N}(X_{0})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho_{0},\rho_{0}^{N}).

As such, the sequence
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
given by
X_{0}^{N}\coloneqq\mathcal{T}^{N}(X_{0})
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, converges to
X_{0}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
with rates inherited from those above for the convergence of
\{\rho_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
to
\rho_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The sequence of partitions
\{\mathcal{O}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\Omega
associated with
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are given by the pre-images
\{X_{0}^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{N})\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of the Laguerre tessellations
\{\mathcal{L}_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of the maps
\{\mathcal{T}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
. The practical construction of the optimal transport maps
\{\mathcal{T}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is an active area of research concerning semi-discrete optimal transport, see
[
19
,
86
,
80
]
for a range of computational approaches.

Example 2.3

(Quantization of random variables)
.

An alternative construction can be achieved through quantization of the random variable
X_{0}
, see the seminal papers
[
66
,
64
]
as well as
[
51
]
for an introduction to the subject. To help formulate the main idea of this approach with an error bound, we introduce some notation. Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{F}_{N}
denote the set of Borel measurable functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
(defined a.e.), where
{\rm card}({\rm Range}(
f
))\leq N
and let the
N
-th quantization error for
\rho_{0}
be defined as

V_{N}(\rho_{0})\coloneqq\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{N}}\|X_{0}-f(X_{0})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

The lower quantization dimension for
\rho_{0}
is defined as

\mathcal{D}_{L}(\rho_{0})\coloneqq\liminf_{N\to\infty}((-2)\log(N)/\log(V_{N}(\rho_{0})))

and the upper quantization dimension for
\rho_{0}
is defined as

\mathcal{D}_{U}(\rho_{0})\coloneqq\limsup_{N\to\infty}((-2)\log(N)/\log(V_{N}(\rho_{0}))).

If
\mathcal{D}_{L}(\rho_{0})=\mathcal{D}_{U}(\rho_{0})
then the common value
\mathcal{D}(\rho_{0})
is called the quantization dimension of
\rho_{0}
. Now, suppose that the initial random variable
X_{0}
has slightly stronger integrability in
L^{2+\delta}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
\delta>0
. Using
[
51
, Theorem 4.1, Theorem 4.12 & Corollary 11.4(c)]
and unpacking the definitions of
\mathcal{D}_{L}(\rho_{0}),\mathcal{D}_{U}(\rho_{0})
, it follows that, for each
\epsilon\in(0,1)
there exist
N_{\dagger}=N_{\dagger}(\epsilon,{\mathcal{D}_{L}(\rho_{0})})\in\mathbb{N}
, sequences of weights
\{\mathcal{A}_{N}\}_{N\geq N_{\dagger}}
, Voronoi tessellations
\{\mathcal{O}_{N}\}_{N\geq N_{\dagger}}
of
\Omega
and maps
\{f_{N}\}_{N\geq N_{\dagger}}
(which achieve the infimum in
V_{N}(\rho_{0})
for each
N
) such that
X_{0}^{N}\coloneqq f_{N}(X_{0})\in\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
for each
N\geq N_{\dagger}
and

N^{-\frac{1}{\mathcal{(}1-\epsilon){D}_{L}(\rho_{0})}}\leq\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\leq N^{-\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{U}(\rho_{0})+\epsilon\mathcal{D}_{L}(\rho_{0})}}\quad\forall N\geq N_{\dagger}(\epsilon,{\mathcal{D}_{L}(\rho_{0})})

(2.30)

where
0<\mathcal{D}_{L}(\rho_{0})\leq\mathcal{D}_{U}(\rho_{0})\leq d
. The above bound merely provides a non-sharp estimate for the asymptotic rate of convergence for such quantizers
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\geq N_{\dagger}}
, where the quantization dimensions need not coincide in general. In the case when
{\rm card}({\rm spt}(\rho_{0}))
is finite the error bound is trivial since then
X_{0}
is already a simple random variable that does not need approximating. If
{\rm card}({\rm spt}(\rho_{0}))=\infty
and
\rho_{0}
has non-trivial absolutely continuous part,
[
51
, Corollary 11.4(c)]
and Zador’s theorem
[
87
]
imply
\mathcal{D}_{L}(\rho_{0})=\mathcal{D}_{U}(\rho_{0})
with
\mathcal{D}(\rho_{0})=d
and one has a more precise bound with a sharp convergence rate:

\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\leq CN^{-1/d}\quad\forall N\geq N_{\dagger}

(2.31)

where the constant
C
depends only on
\delta
,
X_{0}
, the absolutely continuous part of
\rho_{0}
, and
d
. If
\rho_{0}
is a distribution supported on a self-similar subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
then it is possible for
\mathcal{D}_{L}(\rho_{0})=\mathcal{D}_{U}(\rho_{0})
and
\mathcal{D}(\rho_{0})<d
. For example, if
d=1
and
\rho_{0}
is the one-dimensional Cantor distribution,
[
51
, Ch. 14]
gives
\mathcal{D}(\rho_{0})=\log(2)/\log(3)<1
where the optimal dyadic
N_{m}
-quantizer with
N_{m}=2^{m}
satisfies

\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N_{m}}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\leq\frac{1}{8}N_{m}^{-\log(3)/\log(2)}\quad\forall m\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.32)

Concerning the practical construction of quantizers
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, together with the associated weights
\{\mathcal{A}_{N}\}_{N\geq N_{\dagger}}
, points
\{\{x_{j,N}\}_{j=1}^{N}\}_{N\geq N_{\dagger}}
and Voronoi tessellations
\{\mathcal{O}_{N}\}_{N\geq N_{\dagger}}
of
\Omega
, one can consider methods based on Lloyd’s algorithm
[
64
,
57
]
, which include stochastic gradient descent and Newton iterations
[
36
,
78
]
, as well as greedy algorithms
[
65
,
38
]
. In this way, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the deterministic finitely supported measure
\rho_{0}^{N}
is constructed from the quantizer
X_{0}^{N}
and the weights
\mathcal{A}_{N}
, where its support is precisely the range of
X_{0}^{N}
.

The Discrete Problem

The setting for temporal discretization

We introduce some notation for vector-space valued maps that will be used throughout the manuscript.
Let
(A,\|\cdot\|_{A})
be an arbitrary real, normed vector space. Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
be given such that
0\leq a<b
. Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{J}_{k}\coloneqq\{I_{n}\}_{n=1}^{M_{k}}
,
M_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, denote the partition of the time interval
[a,b]
where
I_{n}\coloneqq(t_{n-1},t_{n})
with
t_{n}\coloneqq a+n\tau_{k}
for
n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}
,
t_{0}\coloneqq a
, and the time-step
\tau_{k}\coloneqq(b-a)/M_{k}
, with
M_{k+1}\geq M_{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
M_{k}\rightarrow\infty
as
k\rightarrow\infty
.

The space
\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A)
.

•

We let
\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A)
denote the vector space of
A
-valued functions defined on
(a,b)
that are piecewise constant-in-time with respect to the temporal partition
\mathcal{J}_{k}
, i.e.
X\in\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A)
if and only if
X|_{I_{n}}\in A
is constant in time for all
n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}
.

•

For a function
X\in\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A)
and
n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}-1\}
, we let
X(t_{n}^{-})
and
X(t_{n}^{+})
denote respectively the left- and right-limits of
X
in
A
at time
t_{n}
. A function
X\in\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A)
also admits a right-limit
X(t_{0}^{+})=X|_{I_{1}}
in
A
at
t_{0}=a
and a left-limit
X(t_{M_{k}}^{-})=X|_{I_{M_{k}}}
in
A
at
t_{M_{k}}=b
.

Remark 3.1
.

Since
X\in\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A)
is piecewise constant, we clearly have
X(t_{n}^{-})=X|_{I_{n}}
in
A
for all
n\in\{1,\dots,M_{k}\}
, and that
X(t_{n}^{+})=X|_{I_{n+1}}
in
A
for all
n\in\{0,\dots,M_{k}-1\}
.

The spaces
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\pm}([a,b];A)
.

In the ensuing analysis it will be useful to consider piecewise constant
A
-valued maps that are defined everywhere in
[a,b]
through either left-continuity or right-continuity. To be precise, we introduce the following notation and conventions.

•

For a function
X\colon([a,b],|\cdot|)\rightarrow(A,\|\cdot\|_{A})
with
X|_{(a,b)}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A)
, we say that
X
is left-continuous if
X(t_{n})=X(t_{n}^{-})
in
A
for all
n=1,\dots,M_{k}
, and that
X
is right-continuous if
X(t_{n})=X(t_{n}^{+})
in
A
for all
n=0,\dots,M_{k}-1
.

•

We define the vector spaces

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([a,b];A)\coloneqq\left\{X\colon[a,b]\rightarrow A\text{ such that\ }X|_{(a,b)}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A),\text{ and }X\text{ is {left}-continuous}\right\},

(3.1a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([a,b];A)\coloneqq\left\{X\colon[a,b]\rightarrow A\text{ such that\ }X|_{(a,b)}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A),\text{ and }X\text{ is {right}-continuous}\right\}.

(3.1b)

•

When
V\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([a,b];A)
and
W\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([a,b];A)
, we set
V(t_{0}^{-})\coloneqq V(a)
in
A
and
W(t_{M_{k}}^{+})\coloneqq W(b)
in
A
.

•

For
V\in\mathbb{V}_{k}(a,b;A)
, we define the jump
\llbracket v\rrbracket_{n}\in A
of
v
at time
t_{n}\in(a,b)
by

\llbracket V\rrbracket_{n}\coloneqq V(t_{n}^{-})-V(t_{n}^{+})\text{ in }A.

(3.2)

We define also the jump
\llbracket V\rrbracket_{0}=V(a)-V(a^{+})
in
A
for
V\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([a,b];A)
, and
\llbracket W\rrbracket_{M_{k}}=W(b^{-})-W(b)
in
A
for
W\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([a,b];A)
.

Throughout this work, we adopt the convention that functions in
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\pm}([a,b];A)
are defined at all points in
[a,b]
, and that two functions in
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\pm}([a,b];A)
that agree up to a subset of measure zero of
[a,b]
are
not
identified.

Reconstruction of time derivatives.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
be given such that
0\leq a<b
. We let
C([a,b];A)
be the space of continuous maps from
([a,b],|\cdot|)
to
(A,\|\cdot\|_{A})
. The formulation of the numerical scheme in the next section will be based on the following interpolation-in-time of maps from
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\pm}([a,b];A)
:

•

Let the
forward-in-time
reconstruction operator
\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}:\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([a,b];A)\to{C([a,b];A)}
be defined by

(\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V)(t)\coloneqq{V(t)+\frac{t_{n}-t}{\tau_{k}}\llbracket V\rrbracket_{n-1}}\text{ in }A,\text{ }t\in{(t_{n-1},t_{n}]},\;n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\},V\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([a,b];A).

(3.3)

•

Define the
backward-in-time
reconstruction operator
\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}:\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([a,b];A)\to C([a,b];A)
by

(\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W)(t)\coloneqq{W(t)-\frac{t-t_{n-1}}{\tau_{k}}\llbracket W\rrbracket_{n}}\text{ in }A,\text{ }{t\in[t_{n-1},t_{n})},\;n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\},W\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([a,b];A).

(3.4)

Throughout, we slightly abuse notation by not indicating the dependence of
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}^{k}
on
A
as there is no risk of confusion.

Remark 3.2
.

Since
V\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([a,b];A)
is by definition left-continuous, it follows that
\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V
is continuous on
[a,b]
and satisfies
\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V(t_{n})=V(t_{n})=V(t_{n}^{-})
in
A
for all
n\in\{0,1,2,\dots,M_{k}\}
and
\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V(a)=V(a)
in
A
. Likewise, for
W\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([a,b];A)
, the map
\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W
is continuous on
[a,b]
and
\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W(t_{n})=W(t_{n})=W(t_{n}^{+})
in
A
for all
n\in\{0,\dots,M_{k}\}
and
\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W(b)=W(b)
in
A
.

It can be shown that
\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V
and
\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W
are piecewise continuously differentiable with respect to the time partition
\mathcal{J}_{k}
for any
V\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([a,b];A)
and any
W\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([a,b];A)
, where

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V|_{I_{n}}=-\frac{1}{\tau_{k}}\llbracket V\rrbracket_{n-1},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W|_{I_{n}}=-\frac{1}{\tau_{k}}\llbracket W\rrbracket_{n},\text{ in }A

\displaystyle\forall n\in\{1,\dots,M_{k}\}.

(3.5)

This shows that
\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}
and
\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}
are respectively related to the first-order backward and forward difference operators.

Discrete-time probability measure flows.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be given. We let
\mathcal{P}_{d,N}
denote the set of deterministic, finitely supported probability measures of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}\delta_{{y}_{i}}
where
\{{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:i=1,\dots,N\}
and
\{\alpha_{i,N}\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset(0,1)
are given such that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}=1
. We then introduce the set of maps

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{k}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{d,N})\coloneqq\Big\{&\upsilon:[0,T]\to(\mathcal{P}_{d,N},\mathcal{W}_{2})\text{ s.t.\ }\upsilon|_{I_{n}}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ constant }\forall n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}\\
&\text{ and }\upsilon\text{ is left-continuous}\Big\},\end{split}

(3.6)

which thus consist of deterministic, empirical probability measure flows that are piecewise constant in time with respect to the time partition
\mathcal{J}_{k}
that are defined everywhere in
[0,T]
. Note that when
\varrho\in\mathbb{P}_{k}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{d,N})
we set
\varrho(t_{0}^{-})\coloneqq\varrho(0)
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Discretization of the Hamiltonian system

The numerical scheme.

We now present a numerical scheme for the approximation of the characteristic system of the first-order MFG system (
1.1
). For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\rho_{0}^{N}
denote an arbitrary deterministic finitely supported measure approximation of
\rho_{0}
of the form
\rho_{0}^{N}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}\delta_{x_{i,N}}
where
\{x_{i,N}\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
X_{0}^{N}
denote the corresponding
\rho_{0}^{N}
-distributed random variable in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
.

The numerical method we consider in this work is defined as follows:
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
be given. Find
X^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
,
Y^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
and
\rho^{k,N}\in\mathbb{P}_{k}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{d,N})
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}X^{k,N}(t)

\displaystyle=X_{0}^{N}+\int_{0}^{t}D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N}(s),Y^{k,N}(s),\rho^{k,N}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s,\ \forall t\in[0,T],

(3.7a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}Y^{k,N}(t)

\displaystyle=-D_{x}g\left(X^{k,N}(T),\rho^{k,N}(T)\right)+\int_{t}^{T}D_{x}H\left(X^{k,N}(s),Y^{k,N}(s),\rho^{k,N}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s,\ \forall t\in[0,T],

(3.7b)

\displaystyle\rho^{k,N}(t)

\displaystyle=(X^{k,N}(t))_{\#}\mathbb{P},\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

(3.7c)

Remark 3.3
.

In view of Remark
2.1
, whenever
\rho_{0}
is an measure supported on a finite number of points with corresponding simple random variable
X_{0}
we consider the constant sequence
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of initial conditions given by
X_{0}^{N}\coloneqq X_{0}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the numerical scheme (
3.7
).

Remark 3.4
.

The integral terms in each equation (
3.7a
), (
3.7b
) are well-defined. Indeed, write
X^{k,N}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}
and
Y^{k,N}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}Y^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}
for each
t\in[0,T]
. Since
X^{k,N}(t)
is piecewise constant-in-time with values in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
and is left-continuous as maps from
([0,T],|\cdot|)
to
(\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}),\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})})
, we have

\begin{split}\rho^{k,N}(t)&=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}\delta_{X^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}}\end{split}

(3.8)

is a deterministic map from
([0,T],|\cdot|)
to
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),W_{2})
that is piecewise constant in time. To see that
\rho^{k,N}
is left-continuous, notice that there holds the bound

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho^{k,N}(s),\rho^{k,N}(r))\leq\|X^{k,N}(s)-X^{k,N}(r)\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}\quad\forall s,r\in[0,T]

(3.9)

since
\rho^{k,N}(t)
is the law of
X^{k,N}(t)
with respect to the reference probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
for each
t\in[0,T]
. It then follows that
\rho^{k,N}
is left-continuous on
[0,T]
with
\rho^{k,N}(t_{0}^{-})=\rho_{0}^{N}=\rho(0)
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that
\rho^{k,N}
is in
\mathbb{P}_{k}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{d,N})
.
We then obtain that the map
[0,T]\ni t\mapsto D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N}(t),Y^{k,N}(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)
is piecewise constant in time and defined via

D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N}(t),Y^{k,N}(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)|_{I_{n}}=D_{p}H\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}},\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}},\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}\delta_{\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)}\right),

(3.10)

for
t\in I_{n}
, where
\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)\coloneqq X^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}
and
\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)\coloneqq Y^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}
are constant for
t\in I_{n}
since
X^{k,N},Y^{k,N}
are constant-in-time on
I_{n}
for
n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}
. It further follows that, for
n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}
and
\omega\in\Omega_{j,N}
where
i=1,2,\cdots,N
,

\displaystyle D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N}(t)(\omega),Y^{k,N}(t)(\omega),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)|_{I_{n}}=D_{p}H\left(\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{j,N}}|_{I_{n}},\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{j,N}}|_{I_{n}},\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}\delta_{\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}}\right).

Therefore, the map
I_{n}\ni s\mapsto D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N}(t),Y^{k,N}(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)
has values in the vector space of uniform random variables on
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
where

\displaystyle D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N}(t),Y^{k,N}(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)|_{I_{n}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}D_{p}H\left(\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{j,N}}|_{I_{n}},\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{j,N}}|_{I_{n}},\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}\delta_{\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}}\right)\chi_{\Omega_{j,N}}

(3.11)

in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
for each
n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}
, so
D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N},Y^{k,N},\rho^{k,N}\right)
is a map in
\mathbb{V}_{k}(0,T;\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
and the integral in (
3.7a
) is therefore well-defined as it is the integral of a simple function in time with values in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
. Similarly,
D_{x}H\left(X^{k,N},Y^{k,N},\rho^{k,N}\right)
is in
\mathbb{V}_{k}(0,T;\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
where

\displaystyle D_{x}H\left(X^{k,N}(t),Y^{k,N}(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)|_{I_{n}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}D_{x}H\left(\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{j,N}}|_{I_{n}},\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{j,N}}|_{I_{n}},\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}\delta_{\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}}\right)\chi_{\Omega_{j,N}}

(3.12)

in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
for all
t\in I_{n}
,
n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}
,
so the integral in (
3.7b
) is well-defined as it is the integral of a simple function in time with values in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
.

Remark 3.5

(A numerical approximation for the value function)
.

The numerical scheme (
3.7
) also implies an immediate construction of approximations for the value function (
2.23
) evaluated along the optimal trajectory. Indeed, given
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
, if
(X^{k,N},Y^{k,N},\rho^{k,N})
satisfies the discrete system (
3.7
), define the map
u^{k,N}\in C([0,T];L^{1}(\Omega))
via

u^{k,N}(t)=\int_{t}^{T}L(X^{k,N}(s),{D_{p}H(X^{k,N}(s),Y^{k,N}(s),\rho^{k,N}(s))},\rho^{k,N}(s))\mathrm{d}s+g(X^{k,N}(T),\rho^{k,N}(T)),\ \forall t\in[0,T].

(3.13)

We note that
u^{k,N}
is indeed
L^{1}(\Omega)
-valued for each
t\in[0,T]
due to Fubini’s theorem and the fact that
X^{k,N}
,
Y^{k,N}
are piecewise constant in
(0,T)\times\Omega
and
\rho^{k,N}
is piecewise constant in time.

An equivalent reformulation.

The discrete scheme (
3.7
) can be viewed as a probabilistric interpretation of the following problem:
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
be given. Find maps
\mathcal{X}_{k,N}\in[\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})]^{N}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}\in[\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})]^{N}
, and
\rho^{k,N}\in\mathbb{P}_{k}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{d,N})
of the form
\mathcal{X}_{k,N}(t)=(\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t))_{i=1}^{N}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}(t)=(\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(t))_{i=1}^{N}
where
\rho^{k,N}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}\delta_{\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)}
in
[0,T]
, and for each
i\in\{1,2,\cdots,N\}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)

\displaystyle=D_{p}H\left(\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t),\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right),

\displaystyle t\in(0,T)\backslash\iota_{k},

(3.14a)

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(t)

\displaystyle=D_{x}H\left(\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t),\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right),

\displaystyle t\in(0,T)\backslash\iota_{k},

(3.14b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(0)

\displaystyle=x_{i,N},\quad\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(T)=-D_{x}g(\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(T),\rho^{k,N}(T)),

(3.14c)

where
\iota_{k}\coloneqq\{t_{m}:q=1,2,\cdots,M_{k}-1\}
.

The following result shows that the numerical scheme (
3.7
) can be formulated in a mesh-free manner through the above formulation (
3.14
), which benefits the practical implementation of (
3.7
) .

Proposition 3.1

(Equivalence of numerical schemes)
.

The schemes (
3.7
) and (
3.14
) are equivalent in the following sense :

X^{k,N}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}},\quad Y^{k,N}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(t)\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}\quad\text{ in }\quad\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})\quad\forall t\in[0,T].

(3.15)

For the sake of brevity of the presentation, we include the proof of this proposition in Appendix
A
.

Main results

The first main result concerns the existence of solutions to the numerical scheme (
3.7
).

Theorem 4.1

(Existence of numerical approximations)
.

Let the Hamiltonian
H
satisfy the regularity assumptions (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.5
), (
2.6
), and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.8
). Let the terminal cost
g
satisfy the regularity assumptions (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.12
). For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
X_{0}^{N}
denote an initial random variable in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
for some set of weights
\mathcal{A}_{N}
and a partition
\mathcal{O}_{N}
of
\Omega
. Then, there exists
k_{\dagger}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for each
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq k_{\dagger}
, there exist
X^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
,
Y^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
and
\rho^{k,N}\in\mathbb{P}_{k}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{d,N})
which satisfy the numerical scheme (
3.7
). The constant
k_{\dagger}
depends only on the time horizon
T
, the uniform convexity constant
c_{0}>0
, and the Lipschitz constants associated with
D_{p}H
,
D_{x}H
and
D_{x}g
.

Moreover, we have the uniqueness of such solutions, as the following result shows.

Theorem 4.2

(Uniqueness of numerical approximation)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
. Let
k_{\dagger}\in\mathbb{N}
be given as in Theorem
4.1
. Suppose that for each
k,N\in\mathbb{N}
, with
k\geq k_{\dagger}
, we are given triples
(X_{i}^{k,N},Y_{i}^{k,N},\rho_{i}^{k,N})
,
i=1,2
, that satisfy the scheme (
3.7
) with initial random variables
X_{0,i}^{N}\in\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
for
i=1,2
. Then

\begin{split}&\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\rho_{1}^{k,N}(t),\rho_{2}^{k,N}(t)\right)+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\|(Y_{1}^{k,N}-Y_{2}^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|(X_{1}^{k,N}-X_{2}^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\lesssim\lVert X_{0,1}^{N}-X_{0,2}^{N}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\end{split}

(4.1)

where
k_{\dagger}
and the hidden constant above depend only on the time horizon
T
, the uniform convexity constant
c_{0}>0
, and the Lipschitz constants associated with
D_{p}H
,
D_{x}H
and
D_{x}g
. In particular, the numerical scheme (
3.7
) admits a unique solution for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

The proofs of Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
4.2
are given in Section
6
and Section
5
, respectively. In both instances, the analysis we present is based on a continuous dependence bound that we prove in Theorem
5.1
of Section
5
for a class of discrete Hamiltonian systems under displacement monotonicity.

Furthermore, we prove the convergence of the numerical scheme (
3.7
) together with an asymptotic error bound for the approximations.

Theorem 4.3

(Convergence of numerical approximations)
.

In addition to the hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
, assume that the Hamiltonian satisfies (
2.7
) and that the Lagrangian
L
and
g
satisfy the growth assumption (
2.16
). Let
X_{0}
be an initial random variable distributed according to
\rho_{0}
and assume that
X_{0}^{N}\to X_{0}
in
{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}
as
N\to\infty
. Furthermore, let
(X,Y,\rho)\in C^{1}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times C^{1}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
denote the triple which uniquely satisfies the continuous Hamiltonian system (
2.19
) with initial random variable
X_{0}
. Then, there exists
k_{\dagger}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\rho(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(-D_{x}u(\cdot,X)-Y^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(X-X^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
\lesssim\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}+\tau_{k}

(4.2)

for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\geq k_{\dagger}
,
where
k_{\dagger}
and the hidden constant above depend only on the time horizon
T
, the uniform convexity constant
c_{0}>0
, and the Lipschitz constants associated with
D_{p}H
,
D_{x}H
and
D_{x}g
. In particular,

Y^{k,N}\to Y=-D_{x}u(\cdot,X)\text{ in }L^{\infty}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})),\quad\text{ and }\quad\rho^{k,N}\to\rho\text{ in }L^{\infty}([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

(4.3)

as
k,N\to\infty
.

If in addition
L
and
g
are locally Lipschitz continuous in the measure variable in the sense of (
2.7
) and (
2.10
), we obtain an error estimate for the value function approximation
u^{k,N}
given by (
3.13
).

Theorem 4.4

(Approximation of the value function)
.

Let
u
denote the value function in the continuous MFG, represented along optimal trajectories in (
2.23
) and let
u^{k,N}
stand for the discretised value function defined in (
3.13
).
In addition to the hypotheses of Theorem
4.3
, assume that
L
and
g
satisfy (
2.27a
) and (
2.27b
), respectively. Then, there exist
k_{\dagger}^{*},N_{\dagger}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(u(\cdot,X)-u^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}+\tau_{k},

(4.4)

where
u^{k,N}
is defined by (
3.13
), for each
N\geq N_{\dagger}
and
k\geq k_{\dagger}^{*}
.

The proofs of Theorem
4.3
and Theorem
4.4
are given in Section
7
.

Remark 4.1
.

For the case when the initial measure
\rho_{0}
is an measure supported on a finite number of points, the sequence
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
can be taken to be constant and equal to
X_{0}
(see Remark
3.3
), and so the spatial error term
\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}
is not present in the error bounds (
4.2
),(
4.4
) for all
k
sufficiently large. Thus, the asymptotic rate of convergence of the numerical scheme is order
\tau_{k}
, which is optimal in view of the
H^{1}
-regularity in time for both the state flow
X
and momentum flow
Y
when
\rho_{0}
is supported on a finite number of points. Otherwise, the spatial error term in (
4.2
) is non-zero in general when
\text{card}(\text{spt}(\rho_{0}))=\infty
.

As a first corollary of Theorem
4.3
, we deduce rates of convergence for the numerical scheme (
3.7
) when the approximations
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are given by application of suitable discrete optimal transport maps to
X_{0}
, as illustrated in Example
2.2
.

Corollary 4.5
.

In addition to the hypothesis of Theorem
4.3
, suppose that
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for some
q>2
, is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{P}
. Furthermore, let
(X,Y,\rho)\in C^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times C^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
denote the triple which uniquely satisfies the continuous Hamiltonian system (
2.19
) with initial random variable
X_{0}
distributed according to
\rho_{0}
. Suppose that the sequences
\{\rho_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are constructed as in Example
2.2
. Then, there exists
k_{\dagger}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\rho(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(Y-Y^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(X-X^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
\lesssim\begin{cases}\max\{N^{-1/d},N^{-1/2+1/q}\}+\tau_{k},&{\rm if\ }d\neq\frac{2q}{q-2},\\
N^{-1/d}\log(1+N)^{1/d}+\tau_{k},&{\rm if\ }d=\frac{2q}{q-2},\end{cases}

(4.5)

for all
k\geq k_{\dagger}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. The hidden constant above depends only on
M_{q}(\rho_{0})
, the time horizon
T
, the uniform convexity constant
c_{0}>0
, and the Lipschitz constants associated with
D_{p}H
,
D_{x}H
and
D_{x}g
. If in addition
L
and
g
satisfy (
2.27a
) and (
2.27b
), respectively, then

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(u(\cdot,X)-u^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\begin{cases}\max\{N^{-1/d},N^{-1/2+1/q}\}+\tau_{k},&{\rm if\ }d\neq\frac{2q}{q-2},\\
N^{-1/d}\log(1+N)^{1/d}+\tau_{k},&{\rm if\ }d=\frac{2q}{q-2},\end{cases}

(4.6)

for all
k,N
sufficiently large.

In view of Example
2.3
, we obtain an explicit rate of convergence in the alternative case where the approximations
\{X_{0}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
are constructed via quantization of
X_{0}
.

Corollary 4.6
.

In addition to the hypothesis of Theorem
4.3
, suppose that
\rho_{0}
has a non-trivial absolutely continuous part, and that the initial random variable
X_{0}
is in
L^{2+\delta}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for some
\delta>0
. Furthermore, let
(X,Y,\rho)\in C^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times C^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
