-norms for computing the one-step error quantities (namely
E_{1},E_{local},E_{global}
) in each of the algorithms is a choice made merely for formulating the algorithms; for each algorithm one can fix any norm to check the stated convergence criteria since all norms are equivalent in the finite dimensional spaces
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{\pm}
.

(X_{j}^{k,N}(0),Y_{j}^{k,N}(0))
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{1})
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(0)
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{1})
Picard iter.

on
[0,t_{1}]

(X_{j}^{k,N}(t_{n}),Y_{j}^{k,N}(t_{n}))
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n+1})
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n})
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n+1})
Picard iter.

on
[t_{n},t_{n+1}]
(X_{j}^{k,N}(t_{M_{k}-1}),Y_{j}^{k,N}(t_{M_{k}-1}))
\hskip 8.50012pt\mathcal{T}=-D_{x}g
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{M_{k}-1})
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(T)
Picard iter.

on
[t_{M_{k}-1},T]
\cdots
\cdots

Figure 1
:
Depiction of
j
-th iteration of Algorithm
B
– The direction of the blue arrows indicate downward induction along the temporal subintervals
\{[t_{n},t_{n+1}]\}_{n=0}^{M_{k}-1}
starting from the right-most grey block. Each grey block corresponds to performing standard Picard iteration by Algorithm
A
to approximate the discrete Hamiltonian system posed on the
n
-th subinterval
[t_{n},t_{n+1}]
with initialization being the value pair
(X_{j}^{k,N}(t_{n}),Y_{j}^{k,N}(t_{n}))
(red arrow) and output pair being the pair
(X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n+1}),Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n}))
(magenta and blue arrows respectively). Communication between subintervals is done by setting the terminal time value of
Y
on the
n
-th interval as the
Y
-component of the output of the
(n+1)
-th interval, where for
n=M_{k}-1
the terminal time value
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(T)
is implicitly determined by
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(T)
via the terminal time coupling
\mathcal{T}=-D_{x}g
. With
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(0)\coloneqq X_{0}^{N}
, the nodal output values of one downward sweep completely determine the function pairs
(X_{j+1}^{k,N},Y_{j+1}^{k,N})
on
[0,T]
used as input for the next
(j+1)
-th iteration of the algorithm.

Proving the convergence of Algorithm
B
is outside the scope of the current paper and will be the subject of future research. Nonetheless, the following result sheds light on the relationship between Algorithm
B
and the target numerical scheme (
3.7
) in an ideal scenario where all of the local Picard iterations of the algorithm are solved exactly, i.e. with zero one-step error.

Proposition 8.1

(An a posteriori error bound for Algorithm
B
)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
. Let
k\geq k_{\dagger}
be given, where
k_{\dagger}\in\mathbb{N}
is as in Theorem
4.1
and let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be given. Suppose that in Algorithm
B
the computation of the map
\Phi^{k,N}
is always exact, in the sense that the first one-step error made in employing Algorithm
A
to compute
\Phi^{k,N}
is always equal to zero. Then, the sequence of approximations
\{(X_{j+1}^{k,N},Y_{j+1}^{k,N})\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
generated by Algorithm
B
satisfies the discrete Hamiltonian system

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}{X}_{j+1}^{k,N}(t)&=D_{p}H\left({X}_{j+1}^{k,N}(t),{Y}_{j+1}^{k,N}(t),(X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t))_{\#}\mathbb{P}\right)+F_{j}(t)\quad{\rm in\ }\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})&&\forall t\in(0,T)\backslash\iota_{k},\\
\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}{Y}_{j+1}^{k,N}(t)&=-D_{x}H\left({X}_{j+1}^{k,N}(t),{Y}_{j+1}^{k,N}(t),(X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t))_{\#}\mathbb{P}\right)\quad{\rm in\ }\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})&&\forall t\in(0,T)\backslash\iota_{k},\\
{X}_{j+1}^{k,N}(0)&=X_{0}^{N},\quad{Y}_{j+1}^{k,N}(T)=-D_{x}g({X}_{j+1}^{k,N}(T),(X_{j+1}^{k,N}(T))_{\#}\mathbb{P})\quad{\rm in\ }\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})&&\text{ }\end{aligned}\right.

(8.1)

where
F_{j}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}(0,T;\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
is a residual error term defined by
F_{j}|_{I_{1}}=0
in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
and

F_{j}|_{I_{n}}\coloneqq\tau_{k}^{-1}\left(X_{j}^{k,N}(t_{n-1})-X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n-1})\right)

(8.2)

in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
for
n\in\{2,3,\cdots,M_{k}\}
. Furthermore, there holds the error bound

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left(\lVert({X}^{k,N}-{X}_{j+1}^{k,N})(t)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\lVert({Y}^{k,N}-{Y}_{j+1}^{k,N})(t)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)\lesssim\|F_{j}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})})}

(8.3)

where the hidden constant in (
8.3
) depends only on the time horizon
T
, the uniform convexity constant
c_{0}>0
, and the Lipschitz constants of
D_{p}H
,
D_{x}H
and
D_{x}g
.

Proof.

We give a sketch of the proof as each step consists of merely direct calculations. Since we have assumed that
\Phi^{k,N}
is always exactly evaluated, we have from Algorithm
B
that, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n})=X_{j}^{k,N}(t_{n-1})+\tau_{k}D_{p}H\left(X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n}),Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n-1}),(X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n}))_{\#}\mathbb{P}\right)

(8.4)

Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n-1})=Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n})+\tau_{k}D_{x}H\left(X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n}),Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n-1}),(X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n}))_{\#}\mathbb{P}\right)

(8.5)

for all
n\in\{1,2,\cdots,M_{k}\}
where
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(T)=-D_{x}g({X}_{j+1}^{k,N}(T),(X_{j+1}^{k,N}(T))_{\#}\mathbb{P})
and
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(0)=X_{0}^{N}
. There are only two unknowns of the local forward-backward Hamiltonian system on
(t_{n-1},t_{n})
, namely the values
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n})
and
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n-1})
, whereas
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n})
is known from the previous local forward-backward Hamiltonian system on the subinterval
(t_{n},t_{n+1})
and
X_{j}^{k,N}(t_{n-1})
is known from the previous global iteration. The perturbed forward-backward Hamiltonian system (
8.1
) with residual term (
8.2
) is then obtained by directly computing the partial derivatives
\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}{X}_{j+1}^{k,N}(t)
,
\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}{Y}_{j+1}^{k,N}(t)
for
t\in(0,T)\backslash\iota_{k}
by using the definition of the interpolation operators
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}^{k}
and the equations (
8.4
), (
8.5
), in similar fashion to the proof of Lemma
6.2
. Because
k\geq k_{\dagger}
, we can apply our main continuous dependence bound from Theorem
5.1
to the numerical scheme (
3.7
) and the system (
8.1
) obtained from Algorithm
B
to immediately get the claimed a posteriori error bound
(
8.3
).
∎

Algorithm B

Local Picard Iteration Method for the discrete Hamiltonian System (
3.7
)

k,N\in\mathbb{N}
fixed;
j=0
;
0<tol_{global},tol_{local}<1
;
X_{init}^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
with
X_{init}^{k,N}(0)=X_{0}^{N}
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
Y_{init}^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
;
E_{global}=1
and
E_{local}=1
.

Set
X_{0}^{k,N}\coloneqq X_{init}^{k,N}
in
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
,
Y_{0}^{k,N}\coloneqq Y_{init}^{k,N}
in
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
.

while

E_{global}>tol_{global}

do

Use
X_{j}^{k,N}
and
Y_{j}^{k,N}
to compute
X_{j+1}^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
with
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(0)\coloneqq X_{0}^{N}
in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
,
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N}))
as follows:

Computation on subinterval
[t_{M_{k}-1},T]

Set
\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})\to\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
via
\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{X}]\coloneqq-D_{x}g\left(\mathcal{X},{\mathcal{X}}_{\#}\mathbb{P}\right)
,
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
.

Compute
(\Phi_{X},\Phi_{Y})\coloneqq\Phi^{k,N}[t_{M_{k}-1},T,tol_{local},X_{j}^{k,N},Y_{j}^{k,N},E_{local},\mathcal{T}]
by Algorithm
A
.

Set
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(T)\coloneqq\Phi_{X}(T)
,
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(T)\coloneqq\Phi_{Y}(T)
,
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{M_{k}-1})\coloneqq\Phi_{Y}(t_{M_{k}-1})
in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
.

for

i=M_{k}-2,M_{k}-3,\cdots,3,2,1

do

Computation on subinterval
[t_{n},t_{n+1}]

Set the constant map
\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})\to\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
via
\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{X}]\coloneqq Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n+1})
,
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
.

Compute
(\Phi_{X},\Phi_{Y})\coloneqq\Phi^{k,N}[t_{n},t_{n+1},tol_{local},X_{j}^{k,N},Y_{j}^{k,N},E_{local},\mathcal{T}]
by Algorithm
A
.

Set
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n+1})\coloneqq\Phi_{X}(t_{n+1})
,
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{n})\coloneqq\Phi_{Y}(t_{n})
in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
.

end

for

Computation on subinterval
[0,t_{1}]

Set the constant map
\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})\to\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
with
\mathcal{T}[\mathcal{X}]\coloneqq Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{1})
,
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
.

Compute
(\Phi_{X},\Phi_{Y})\coloneqq\Phi^{k,N}[0,t_{1},tol_{local},X_{j}^{k,N},Y_{j}^{k,N},E_{local},\mathcal{T}]
by Algorithm
A
.

Set
X_{j+1}^{k,N}(t_{1})\coloneqq\Phi_{X}(t_{1})
,
Y_{j+1}^{k,N}(0)\coloneqq\Phi_{Y}(0)
in
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
.

Global one-step error update:
Compute

E_{global}=\left\|X_{j+1}^{k,N}-X_{j}^{k,N}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+\left\|Y_{j+1}^{k,N}-Y_{j}^{k,N}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))}.

If
E_{global}>tol_{global}
, set
j+1\leftarrow j
and compute the next global iteration on
[0,T]
.

end

while

The experiments

In this section we present results of two numerical experiments that test the performance of the numerical scheme. To this end, we compare the performance of the standard Picard iteration from Algorithm
A
and the local Picard iteration scheme from Algortihm
B
. Since there holds the estimate

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho(t),\rho^{k,N}(t))\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(X-X^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})},

(8.6)

in the experiments we assess the approximations
(X_{app}^{k,N},Y_{app}^{k,N})
of
(X^{k,N},Y^{k,N})
generated by either algorithm by computing the following relative errors:

E_{X}^{k}\coloneqq\frac{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|(X-X_{app}^{k,N})(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}},\quad E_{Y}^{k}\coloneqq\frac{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|Y-Y_{app}^{k,N}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|Y(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}.

(8.7)

First experiment.

In this test, we consider the approximation of the continuous MFG system with respect to a reference solution computed on a fine grid.
The model data that we consider for the continuous MFG system are as follows. Let the space dimension
d=2
and let
T=1
. Let
\mathcal{S}\coloneqq\{2^{-3}(2m-1,2n-1)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:n,m\in\{1,2,3,4\}\}
, which we note is a set that consists of sixteen distinct points of the unit square
[0,1]^{2}
. Let
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{16}
denote a fixed denumeration of the points in
\mathcal{S}
. We assume that the initial measure
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a deterministic empirical measure of the form
\rho_{0}\coloneqq(1/16)\sum_{i=1}^{16}\delta_{x_{i}}
. The Hamiltonian of the MFG system is taken to be

H(x,p,\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}(p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}-x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2})+\left(p_{1}+p_{2}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(1-\sin(y_{1}+y_{2})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\quad\forall(x,p,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

(8.8)

with the terminal cost set as

g(x,\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2})+\left(x_{1}+x_{2}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(1-\cos(y_{1}+y_{2})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\quad\forall(x,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

(8.9)

From Example
2.1
we find that
H
satisfies the regularity assumptions (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.5
), (
2.6
), (
2.7
) and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.8
), and that the
g
above satisfies the regularity assumptions (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.12
), and that the growth condition (
2.16
) on
g
and the Lagrangian
L
of
H
also holds. In addition, using
[
69
, Theorem 3.7, Theorem 4.5]
, the MFG system (
1.1
) has a unique solution in the sense of
[
69
, Definition 3.2]
.

For the associated continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
), we consider the reference probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
where
\Omega=[0,1]^{2}
,
\mathbb{F}
is the
\sigma
-algebra of Lebesgue measurable subsets of
\Omega
, and
\mathbb{P}
denotes the restriction of the two-dimensional Lebesgue measure to
\Omega
. Let
\mathcal{O}\coloneqq\{\Omega_{i}\}_{i=1}^{16}\subset\Omega
denote a uniform partition of
\Omega
into (open) squares, each with centroid
w_{i}\in\Omega_{i}
and area
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\Omega_{i}})=16^{-1}
, for
i\in\{1,2,\cdots,16\}
. For this problem, we let the initial random variable
X_{0}
take the form
X_{0}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{16}{w_{i}}\chi_{\Omega_{i}}
a.e. in
\Omega
. In this setting we do not have access to an explicit formula for the unique solution
(X,Y)
of the continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
), so instead we consider the performance of the numerical scheme (
3.7
) with respect to the reference solution, which we illustrate in Figure
2
on a coarse temporal grid.

Figure 2
:
First experiment – Illustration of reference solution
(X,Y)
of continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
) with
T=1
on a coarse temporal grid.

In the discrete Hamiltonian system (
3.7
), we simply set
\mathcal{A}_{N}\coloneqq\{\alpha_{i,N}\}_{i=1}^{N}
with
\alpha_{i,N}\coloneqq 1/16
for
i=1,2\cdots,N
,
\mathcal{O}_{N}\coloneq\mathcal{O}
,
\rho_{0}^{N}\coloneqq\rho_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and
X_{0}^{N}\coloneqq X_{0}
in
\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
for fixed
N=16
, since
\rho_{0}
is already an empirical measure supported on a finite number of points. We consider a uniform partition of the time interval
[0,T]
with time-step
\tau_{k}\coloneqq 2^{-k+1}
with
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Theorem
4.3
then implies that the total error
E_{total}^{k}\coloneqq E_{X}^{k}+E_{Y}^{k}
satisfies

E_{total}^{k}\lesssim\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N}\|_{{{L}^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}}+\tau_{k}\lesssim 0+\tau_{k}=\tau_{k}

(8.10)

for sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
. To obtain a reference solution for
(X,Y)
for computing the errors when
T=1
, we applied the standard Picard iteration scheme in Algorithm
A
with
tol_{global}=10^{-8}
,
k_{ref}=12
(whence
\tau_{k_{ref}}=2^{-11}
), and we set
Y_{init}^{k_{ref},N}\coloneq 0
in
\mathbb{V}_{k_{ref}}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}))
and
X_{init}^{k_{ref},N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}))
where
X_{init}^{k_{ref},N}|_{(0,T]}\coloneqq 0
with
X_{init}^{k_{ref},N}(0)\coloneqq X_{0}
. This reference solution is depicted in Figure
2
on a coarser temporal partition. In the next sub-test where we let
T\in\{2,4,8,16,32\}
, for each
T
we obtain a reference solution
(X,Y)
for computing the errors by employing the local Picard iteration of Algorithm
B
with the same initialisation
X_{init}^{k_{ref},N}
,
Y_{init}^{k_{ref},N}
, where
k_{ref}=12
and global tolerance
tol_{global}=10^{-8}
, but we set the local tolerance to be
tol_{local}=10^{-12}
.

For
k\in\{2,3,\cdots,9\}
, we apply both the standard Algorithm

and the local Algorithm

in MATLAB to solve the discrete Hamiltonian system (

), where for each algorithm we initialized by setting

in

and
X_{init}^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}))
where

with

, and we set the global tolerance to be

. The local error tolerance for Algorithm
B
was set to
tol_{local}=10^{-12}
. For solving the associated discrete McKean–Vlasov equations using Algorithm
MKV
in the respective implementations, throughout we initialized using
X_{init}^{k,N}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{O}))
where
X_{init}^{k,N}|_{(0,T]}\coloneqq 0
with
X_{init}^{k,N}(0)\coloneqq\chi
where
\chi
denotes a generic initial condition that is determined by the given scheme (i.e. being fixed for Algorithm
A
but varying in Algorithm
B
across subinterval computations). The algorithm for approximating the discrete McKean–Vlasov equation was run with error tolerance
tol=10^{-12}
throughout both implementations of Algorithm
B
and Algorithm
A
in this experiment.

The error plots given by the discrete system (
3.7
) for this experiment are shown in Figure
3
. We observe that the error plots indicate an asymptotic rate of convergence of order one as the time-step vanishes, which is in agreement with our theoretical prediction. This observed rate of convergence is optimal since the approximation is based on piecewise constant-in-time approximations of
H^{1}
-regular time curves. We further see that the approximations generated by both Algorithms
A
and
B
produce comparable discrete solutions since their respective error plots overlap well.

We also assess the robustness of the numerical scheme (
3.7
) for longer time horizons
T\in\{2,4,8,16,32\}
by using Algorithm
B
based on local Picard iterations. We assess here robustness in the sense that the asymptotic rate of convergence of the method is independent of the time horizon
T
. The relative errors are plotted in Figure
4
. We observe in particular the decrease in the number of points in the curves from eight points to three points as
T
increases. We suspect that this is due to Algorithm
B
requiring sufficiently large mesh parameter
k_{\dagger}
to converge to the approximation
(X^{k,N_{k}},Y^{k,N_{k}})
for each given
k\geq k_{\dagger}
for the entire range of time horizons
T\in\{1,2,4,8,16,32\}
. For instance, in the experiments for
T=32
we observed non-convergence of the algorithm for each
k<k_{\dagger}
with
k_{\dagger}=6
. This size condition on
k
for this experiment is perhaps not surprising, since there is a similar condition on
k
in the statements of Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
4.2
on well-posedness for the numerical scheme. We see that the asymptotic slope of each error plot is comparable to that of the base case
T=1
. Therefore, these results suggest that the numerical scheme (
3.7
) is robust as the time horizon increases.

Second experiment.

In this experiment we test the performance of the numerical scheme (
3.7
) for a MFG system with an exact solution.
We consider the continuous MFG system (
1.1
) with the following model data. We let the space dimension
d=2
, the time horizon
T\in(0,\infty)
and we consider the initial measure
\rho_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\rho_{0}(x)\coloneqq\chi_{[0,1]^{2}}(x)
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Note that, the second moment of
\rho_{0}
is
M_{2}(\rho_{0})=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}{\rho}_{0}(x)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}
. We set the Hamiltonian
H(x,p,\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}|p|^{2}
,
(x,p,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the terminal cost to be
g(x,\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}|x|^{2}M_{2}^{2}(\mu)
,
(x,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

It is clear that
H
satisfies the regularity assumptions (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.5
), (
2.6
) and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.8
). Furthermore,
g
satisfies (
2.9
) and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.12
) (to remain self-contained in our analysis – as this is not a consequence of the results in Example
2.1
– we supply the proof of this fact in Lemma
A.1
). However, the derivative
D_{x}g
is only locally Lipschitz continuous in the measure variable, and so does not satisfy the uniform Lipschitz continuity assumption (
2.10
). Nonetheless, we now show by construction that the MFG system (
1.1
) has a solution
(u,\rho)
, whence the solution is unique by arguments in
[
69
, Corollary 4.3]
due to displacement monotonicity of the data. Suppose we are given a distribution curve
\tilde{\rho}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Then the unique viscosity solution
\tilde{u}
of the Hamilton–Jacobi equation of the system (
1.1
) for given distribution
\tilde{\rho}
takes the form

\tilde{u}(t,x)=\inf_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{4}|y|^{2}M_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T))+\frac{1}{2(T-t)}|x-y|^{2}\right)\quad\forall(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

(8.11)

due to the Hopf–Lax formula.
The infimum here is uniquely attained at

y_{*}(t,x)=\frac{2}{2+(T-t)M_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T))}x,\quad\forall(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2},

(8.12)

which yields the value function ansatz

\tilde{u}(t,x)=\frac{|x|^{2}M_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T))}{2(2+(T-t)M_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T)))},\quad\forall(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(8.13)

We then have that

D_{x}\tilde{u}(t,x)=\frac{M_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T))}{2+(T-t)M_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T))}x,\quad\forall(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

(8.14)

This gradient results in the player distribution ansatz
{\rho}_{*}(t)\coloneqq(\mathcal{X}(t))_{\#}\rho_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
, where the flow
(\mathcal{X}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
is uniquely given by the ODE

\frac{d\mathcal{X}}{dt}(t,x)=-D_{x}\tilde{u}(t,\mathcal{X}(t)),\quad t\in[0,T],\quad\mathcal{X}(0)=x,\quad\forall x\in\text{spt}(\rho_{0})=[0,1]^{2}.

(8.15)

By a direct calculation we obtain

\mathcal{X}(t,x)=\frac{2+(T-t)M_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T))}{2+TM_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T))}x,\quad(t,x)\in[0,T]\times[0,1]^{2}.

(8.16)

Furthermore, using
[
69
, Lemma 3.6]
, we have that
\rho_{*}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is the unique distributional solution to the Cauchy problem
\partial_{t}\rho_{*}-D_{x}\cdot\left(\rho_{*}D_{x}\tilde{u}\right)=0
in
(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\rho_{*}(0)=\rho_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, where
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}M_{2}(\rho_{*}(t))<\infty
. Notice that the ansatz for both
\tilde{u}
and
\rho_{*}
depend on merely the value of
M_{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T))
. As such, we seek a fixed point relation between
M_{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T))
and
M_{2}({\rho}_{*}(T))
which fixes the value of the second-moment of
\rho_{*}
.

Using the definition of the push-forward, we find that

M_{2}^{2}(\rho_{*}(T))=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathcal{X}(T,x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\rho_{0}(x)
and hence

M_{2}^{2}(\rho_{*}(T))=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\mathcal{X}(T,x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\rho_{0}(x)=\frac{4}{(2+TM_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T)))^{2}}M_{2}^{2}(\rho_{0})=\frac{8}{3(2+TM_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\rho}(T)))^{2}}.

(8.17)

Therefore, we arrive at the fixed-point equation

r(T)^{2}=\frac{8}{3(2+Tr(T)^{2})^{2}}

(8.18)

for the second moment of the player distribution. One can show directly that, for each
T\in(0,\infty)
, this equation is satisfied by a unique value of
r(T)\in(0,\sqrt{2/3})
. In addition, it can be shown from (
8.18
) that

r^{2}(T)=\frac{2^{\frac{2}{3}}}{3T}\left(9T+3\sqrt{9T^{2}+4T}+2\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+\frac{2^{\frac{4}{3}}}{T\left(9T+3\sqrt{9T^{2}+4T}+2\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}}-\frac{4}{3T}

(8.19)

for
T\in(0,\infty)
. In all, we have shown that the MFG has a solution
(u,\rho)
where the player distribution is given by
\rho\coloneqq\rho_{*}
with corresponding value function
u\coloneqq\tilde{u}
given by (
8.13
) with
M_{2}(\rho(T))=r(T)
uniquely satisfying (
8.18
). The uniqueness of the solution is then a consequence of
[
69
, Corollary 4.3]
.

As before, we let
\Omega\coloneqq[0,1]^{2}
and we consider the associated continuous Hamiltonian system

\displaystyle X(t)

\displaystyle=X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}Y(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}))

(8.20a)

\displaystyle Y(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{M_{2}^{2}(\rho(T))}{2}X(T),\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}))

(8.20b)

\displaystyle\rho(t)

\displaystyle=(X(t))_{\#}\mathbb{P}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{in }C([0,T];\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})).

(8.20c)

where we let
X_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})
be the identity map
X_{0}(\omega)\coloneqq\omega
,
\omega\in\Omega
, whose range is the support of
\rho_{0}
. Note that
\mathbb{P}
is the restriction of
\rho_{0}
to
[0,1]^{2}
. It follows by a direct calculation that the unique solution of this Hamiltonian system is given by

X(t)=\left(\frac{2+(T-t)M_{2}^{2}(\rho(T))}{2+TM_{2}^{2}(\rho(T))}\right)X_{0},\quad Y(t)=-\frac{M_{2}^{2}(\rho(T))}{2+TM_{2}^{2}(\rho(T))}X_{0}\text{ in }C^{\infty}([0,T];H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))

(8.21)

where
M_{2}(\rho(T))\in(0,\sqrt{2/3})
uniquely satisfies (
8.18
) and is given by (
8.19
). Note that
X(t)=\mathcal{X}(t,X_{0})
and
Y(t)=-D_{x}u(t,X(t))
in
C^{\infty}([0,T];H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Let us formulate the discrete Hamiltonian system that we aim to solve. We begin by setting the discretization parameters. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\mathcal{O}_{N}\coloneqq\{\Omega_{i,N}\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset\Omega
denote a uniform partition of
\Omega
into (open) squares each of area
\mathbb{P}(\overline{\Omega_{i,N}})=N^{-1}
, and we let
X_{0}^{N}\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i,N}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}
where
x_{i,N}
denotes the centroid of
\Omega_{i,N}
. We set
N=N_{k}=2^{2(k-1)}
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and we consider a uniform partition of the time interval
[0,T]
with time-step
\tau_{k}\coloneqq 2^{-k+1}T
with
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In this case, we have that
\tau_{k}\eqsim N_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, and it can be shown from the definition of
X_{0}
and
X_{0}^{N_{k}}
that
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho_{0},\rho_{0}^{N_{k}})\leq\|X_{0}-X_{0}^{N_{k}}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim N_{k}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

\rho
and the value function gradient along the player trajectories, which is given by the flow

for the case

to compare the performance of Algorithm

and Algorithm

. For

, we apply both the standard Algorithm

and the local Algorithm

in MATLAB to solve the discrete system (
3.7
), where for each algorithm we initialised by setting

in

and

. For both algorithms, we set the global tolerance to be
tol_{global}=10^{-8}
with the local error tolerance for Algorithm
B
set as
tol_{local}=10^{-12}
. For solving the associated discrete McKean–Vlasov equations using Algorithm
MKV
in the respective implementations, throughout we initialised using
X_{init}^{k,N_{k}}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}},\mathcal{O}_{N_{k}}))
where
X_{init}^{k,N_{k}}|_{(0,T]}\coloneqq 0
with
X_{init}^{k,N_{k}}(0)\coloneqq\chi
where
\chi
denotes a generic initial condition that is determined by the given scheme (i.e. being fixed for Algorithm
A
but varying in Algorithm
B
), and we set the error tolerance to
tol=10^{-12}
.

3.7
) are shown in Figure

. In this setting where the model data do not satisfy all the hypotheses of Theorem
4.3
, we observe an optimal asymptotic rate of convergence of order one as the time-step vanishes in the error plots. The optimality of this rate of convergence is likely due to the fact that the exact solution of the continuous Hamiltonian system are
H^{1}
-regular in
(0,T)\times\Omega
. Here too we see that the respective error plots given by Algorithm

and Algorithm

overlap well with each other.

In the second part of this experiment, we assess the robustness of the numerical scheme (
3.7
) for longer time horizons
T\in\{2,4,8,16,32\}
by using Algorithm
B
based on local Picard iterations. The relative errors are plotted in Figure
6
. Like in the first experiment, we observe the decrease in the number of points in the curves as
T
increases, namely from eight points to four points in this experiment. In conducting this experiment, we observed convergence of Algorithm
B
for each mesh level
k\geq k_{\dagger}
where
k_{\dagger}=k_{\dagger}(T)
increases from
1
to
5
as
T
increases in the range
T\in\{1,2,4,8,16,32\}
, but otherwise we observed non-convergence of the algorithm for each mesh level
k<k_{\dagger}(T)
. We see that the slope of each error plot is comparable to that of the base case
T=1
, thereby showcasing the robustness of the numerical scheme (
3.7
) as the time horizon increases.

Third experiment.

In this final test, we illustrate the space dimension dependence in the asymptotic rate of convergence implied by Theorem
4.3
for an example model problem. The model data that we consider for the continuous MFG system are as follows. We fix the time horizon to be
T=0.1
and we assume that
d\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
. We let the initial distribution
\rho_{0}
be the regular probability measure given by the standard normal distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with zero mean and identity covariance, i.e.
\rho_{0}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}e^{-|x|^{2}/2}
. Note that
\rho_{0}
is thus supported on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We set the Hamiltonian of the MFG system to be

H(x,p,\mu)\coloneqq\frac{7}{6}(|p|^{2}-|x|^{2})+\left(\phi(p)-\phi(x)\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|z|^{2}}{1+|z|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\quad\forall(x,p,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(8.22)

where the function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
is defined by
\phi(v)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}|v|^{2}-\frac{1}{12}|v|^{4}
if
|v|\leq 1
and
\phi(v)\coloneqq\frac{2}{3}|v|-\frac{1}{4}
if
|v|\geq 1
.
The terminal cost of the system is set as

g(x,\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}|x|^{2}+{(x_{1}+\ldots+x_{d})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(1-\sin({y_{1}+\ldots+y_{d}})\right)\mathrm{d}\mu(y)\quad\forall(x,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(8.23)

From Example
2.1
we find that
H
satisfies the regularity assumptions (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.5
), (
2.6
), (
2.7
) and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.8
), that the
g
above satisfies the regularity assumptions (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.12
), and that the growth condition (
2.16
) on
g
and the Lagrangian
L
of
H
also holds. In addition, using
[
69
, Theorem 3.7, Theorem 4.5]
, the MFG system (
1.1
) has a unique solution in the sense of
[
69
, Definition 3.2]
. For the associated continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
), we consider the reference probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
where
\Omega=[0,1]^{d}
,
\mathbb{F}
is the
\sigma
-algebra of Lebesgue measurable subsets of
\Omega
, and
\mathbb{P}
denotes the
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure restricted to
\Omega
. We let the initial random variable
X_{0}
be distributed according to
\rho_{0}
with
X_{0}(\omega)\coloneqq(\Phi^{-1}(\omega_{1}),\cdots,\Phi^{-1}(\omega_{i}),\cdots,\Phi^{-1}(\omega_{d}))
for almost every
\omega=(\omega_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in\Omega
where
\Phi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
denotes the cumulative distribution function of the one-dimensional standard normal distribution.

For the numerical scheme, we let
N_{k}\coloneqq 2^{k}
and set
\tau_{k}=T/N_{k}
for
k=1,2,3,4,5,6
. Given
k\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7\}
, the approximate random variable
X_{0}^{N_{k}}
is computed in MATLAB as an approximate
N_{k}
-point optimal quantization of
X_{0}
using Lloyd’s algorithm on a Monte Carlo sample of size 1,000,000. We estimate the corresponding quantization weights
\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}}
via computing empirical Voronoi cell frequencies. We do not have access to an explicit formula for the unique solution
(X,Y)
of the continuous Hamiltonian system (
1.3
), so we compute the approximation errors for the numerical scheme (
3.7
) with respect to the reference solution obtained at
N_{ref}=2048
spatial samples with time-step
\tau_{ref}\coloneqq T/N_{ref}
.

For this experiment, we employ the standard Picard iteration via Algorithm
A
to approximate the discrete problem, where we initialised the iteration by setting
Y_{init}^{k,N_{k}}\coloneqq 0
in
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}},\mathcal{O}_{N_{k}}))
and
X_{init}^{k,N_{k}}\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];\mathcal{R}_{2}(\mathcal{A}_{N_{k}},\mathcal{O}_{N_{k}}))
with
X_{init}^{k,N_{k}}|_{(0,T]}\coloneqq 0
and
X_{init}^{k,N_{k}}(0)\coloneqq X_{0}^{N_{k}}
. The iteration was run with the global tolerance set to
tol_{global}=10^{-8}
, while the inner McKean–Vlasov solves were run via Algorithm
MKV
with tolerance
tol=10^{-12}
. For the chosen
N_{k},\tau_{k}
and the fact that
X_{0}\in L^{2+\delta}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every finite
\delta>0
, we have that for each
d\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
Corollary
4.7
gives an error estimate for the scheme of order
N_{k}^{-1/d}+\tau_{k}\eqsim N_{k}^{-1/d}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, with rate of convergence of order
1/d
. In Figure
7
we display error plots for
d\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
, where we observe asymptotic rates of convergence of order
1/d
, in agreement with the conclusion of Corollary
4.7
.

Appendix A
Proofs of auxiliary results

Proof of Lemma
5.3
on discrete integration-by-parts formulae

Proof.

We include only the proof of (
5.9
) as the identities (
5.10
) and (
5.11
) then follow analogously. Let
(V,W)\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be given. Without loss of generality assume that
n>m
in
\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}
so that
t_{n}>t_{m}
. From definition of
\partial_{s}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V
and
\partial_{s}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W
we have via their definitions (
3.5
) that

\begin{split}&\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{s}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V(s)\cdot W(s)+V(s)\cdot\partial_{s}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W(s)\right]{\mathrm{d}s}=\tau_{k}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{s}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V|_{I_{i}}\cdot W|_{I_{i}}+V|_{I_{i}}\cdot\partial_{s}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W|_{I_{i}}\right]\\
&=\tau_{k}\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(-\frac{1}{\tau_{k}}\llbracket V\rrbracket_{i-1}\right)\cdot W|_{I_{i}}+V|_{I_{i}}\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{\tau_{k}}\llbracket W\rrbracket_{i}\right)\right]\\
&=\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left[V|_{I_{i}}-V|_{I_{i-1}}\right]\cdot W|_{I_{i}}+V|_{I_{i}}\cdot\left[W|_{I_{i+1}}-W|_{I_{i}}\right]\right]\\
&=\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[-V|_{I_{i-1}}\cdot W|_{I_{i}}+V|_{I_{i}}\cdot W|_{I_{i+1}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[V|_{I_{n}}\cdot W|_{I_{n+1}}-V|_{I_{m}}\cdot W|_{I_{m+1}}\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[V(t_{n})\cdot W(t_{n})-V(t_{m})\cdot W(t_{m})\right]\end{split}

(A.1)

since
V
is defined everywhere in time through left-continuity in time and
W
is defined everywhere in time through right-continuity in time, by definition of
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, respectively. This proves

\int_{t_{m}}^{t_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{s}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}V(s)\cdot W(s)+V(s)\cdot\partial_{s}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{k}W(s)\right]{\mathrm{d}s}=\mathbb{E}\left[V(t_{n})\cdot W(t_{n})-V(t_{m})\cdot W(t_{m})\right]\quad\forall n,m\in\{0,1,\cdots,M_{k}\}

(A.2)

for all
(V,W)\in\mathbb{V}_{k}^{+}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times\mathbb{V}_{k}^{-}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.6
on discrete Gronwall inequalities

Proof.

Let
k_{\dagger}^{x}
be the smallest positive integer such that
1-c_{x}\tau_{k_{\dagger}^{x}}>0
and fix
k\geq k_{\dagger}^{x}
. We then apply the discrete Gronwall inequality from
[
39
, Proposition 4.1]
to obtain

x_{n}\leq b_{x}(1-c_{x}\tau_{k})^{-n}\quad\forall n\in\{0,1,2,\cdots,M_{k}\}.

(A.3)

By taking
s=c_{x}\tau_{k}/(1-c_{x}\tau_{k})
in the standard estimate
\exp(s)\geq 1+s
,
s\geq 0
, we find that
(1-c_{x}\tau_{k})^{-n}\leq\exp(c_{x}n\tau_{k}/(1-c_{x}\tau_{k}))\leq\exp(c_{x}T/(1-c_{x}\tau_{k_{\dagger}^{x}}))
for all
n\in\{0,1,2,\cdots,M_{k}\}
since
T=M_{k}\tau_{k}
and
\tau_{k}\leq\tau_{k_{\dagger}^{x}}
for all
k\geq k_{\dagger}^{x}
. We thus deduce (
5.57
).

The second bound is obtained via reversal of the index variable under the summation in the hypothesis (
5.58
). Indeed, fix
k\geq k_{*}
. We have from the hypothesis (
5.58
) that

y_{M_{k}-n}\leq b_{y}+c_{y}\tau_{k}\sum_{m=M_{k}-n+1}^{M_{k}}y_{m-1}=b_{y}+c_{y}\tau_{k}\sum_{m=-n}^{-1}y_{M_{k}+m}=b_{y}+c_{y}\tau_{k}\sum_{m=1}^{n}y_{M_{k}-m}

(A.4)

for all
n\in\{0,1,2,\cdots,M_{k}\}
. We let
z_{n}\coloneqq y_{M_{k}-n}
for each
n\in\{0,1,2,\cdots,M_{k}\}
to obtain that

z_{n}\leq b_{y}+c_{y}\tau_{k}\sum_{m=1}^{n}z_{m}\quad\forall n\in\{0,1,2,\cdots,M_{k}\}.

(A.5)

We then apply the discrete Gronwall inequality from
[
39
, Proposition 4.1]
as in the previous case to find that, for each
k\geq k_{\dagger}^{y}
,

\max_{0\leq n\leq M_{k}}y_{n}=\max_{0\leq n\leq M_{k}}z_{n}\leq b_{y}\exp\left(\frac{c_{y}T}{1-c_{y}\tau_{k_{\dagger}^{y}}}\right)

(A.6)

where
k_{\dagger}^{y}
is the smallest positive integer such that
1-c_{x}\tau_{k_{\dagger}^{y}}>0
and
k_{\dagger}^{y}\geq k_{*}
.
∎

Proof of equivalence of numerical schemes

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

If we have a solution to (
3.7
) then the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
implies that we can write the simple random variables as
X^{k,N}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}X^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}
and
Y^{k,N}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}Y^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}
for each
t\in[0,T]
. Therefore, for each
i=1,\cdots,N
, define
\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)\coloneqq X^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(t)\coloneqq Y^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, and note that
\rho^{k,N}(t)=(X^{k,N}(t))_{\#}\mathbb{P}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i,N}\delta_{\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)}

for all
t\in[0,T]
. Now let
n\in\{1,\cdots M_{k}\}
be given. For each
t\in I_{n}
(
3.7a
) and the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}
imply that

{X^{k,N}(t)+\frac{t_{n}-t}{\tau_{k}}\llbracket X^{k,N}(t)\rrbracket_{n-1}}=X_{0}^{N}+\int_{0}^{t}D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N}(s),Y^{k,N}(s),\rho^{k,N}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s

(A.7)

Writing
X^{k,N}(t)|_{I_{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(X^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}\right)|_{I_{n}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)|_{I_{n}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}
where we recall that
\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)\coloneqq X^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}
is constant-in-time on
I_{n}
for
n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}
, we obtain for all
t\in I_{n}

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}+\frac{t_{n}-t}{\tau_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n-1}}-\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}\right)\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}=X_{0}^{N}+\int_{0}^{t}D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N}(s),Y^{k,N}(s),\rho^{k,N}(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s

(A.8)

where we have used the definition of the temporal jumps given in (
3.2
). We differentiate the above w.r.t.
t\in I_{n}
to obtain that

-\tau_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n-1}}-\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}\right)\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}=D_{p}H\left(X^{k,N}(t),Y^{k,N}(t),\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)\quad\forall t\in I_{n}

(A.9)

which is the same as

-\tau_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n-1}}-\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}\right)\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}=D_{p}H\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}},\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}},\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)\quad\forall t\in I_{n}

(A.10)

where we similarly let
Y^{k,N}(t)|_{I_{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}
where
\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)\coloneqq Y^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}
is constant-in-time on
I_{n}
for
n\in\{1,\cdots,M_{k}\}
. Evaluating the above equation at a sample point
\omega\in\Omega_{i,N}
with
i=1,2,\cdots,N
, we then obtain

-\tau_{k}^{-1}\left(\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n-1}}-\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}}\right)=D_{p}H\left(\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}},\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{i,N}}|_{I_{n}},\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)\quad\forall t\in I_{n}.

(A.11)

Since
\mathfrak{X}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)=X^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}=\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)|_{I_{n}}
and
\mathfrak{Y}_{\Omega_{i,N}}(t)=Y^{k,N}(t)|_{\Omega_{i,N}}=\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(t)|_{I_{n}}
, then (
A.11
) is equivalent to

\partial_{t}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{k}\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}|_{I_{n}}=-\tau_{k}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)|_{I_{n-1}}-\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)|_{I_{n}}\right)=D_{p}H\left(\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)|_{I_{n}},\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(t)|_{I_{n}},\rho^{k,N}(t)\right)

(A.12)

for all
t\in I_{n}
. Because this holds for all
t\in I_{n}
,
n=1,\cdots,M_{k}
, we conclude (
3.14a
), as claimed. The derivation of (
3.14b
) follows by a similar direct calculation. It is direct to deduce the initial-terminal condition (
3.14c
) from the scheme (
3.7
) by using the definition of
X_{0}^{N}
and
\mathcal{R}_{d}(\mathcal{A}_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})
. In all, any solution of the scheme (
3.7
) yields a solution to the alternative scheme (
3.14
).

It is then clear from the above calculations that a solution to the first scheme (
3.7
) can be constructed using a solution to the alternative scheme (
3.14
), by defining
X^{k,N}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{X}_{k,N}^{i}(t)\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}
and
Y^{k,N}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{Y}_{k,N}^{i}(t)\chi_{\Omega_{i,N}}
for each
t\in[0,T]
and noting the identities (
3.11
), (
3.12
) and (
3.8
).
∎

Supplementary lemma for second numerical experiment

Lemma A.1
.

The function
g:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathbb{R}
defined as
g(x,\mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}|x|^{2}M_{2}^{2}(\mu)
satisfies the displacement monotonicity condition (
2.12
).

Proof.

We have that
D_{x}g(x,\mu)=\frac{1}{2}xM_{2}^{2}(\mu),
from where for any two random variables
X_{1},X_{2}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\left(D_{x}g({X}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{X_{1}})-D_{x}g({X}_{2},\mathcal{L}_{X_{2}})\right)\cdot({X}_{1}-{X}_{2})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(X_{1}\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]-X_{2}\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}]\right)\cdot({X}_{1}-{X}_{2})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}-X_{2}|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[X_{2}\cdot({X}_{1}-{X}_{2})]\mathbb{E}\left[|X_{1}|^{2}-|X_{2}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]\left(\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}-X_{2}|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[X_{1}\cdot X_{2}]-\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}]\right)-\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}]\left(\mathbb{E}[X_{1}\cdot X_{2}]-\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]\left(\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}\cdot X_{2}]\right)+\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}]\left(-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}\cdot X_{2}]+\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}]\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]\left(\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}]\left(\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}\right)^{2},

where in the penultimate inequality we have used Young’s inequality, i.e.

-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}\cdot X_{2}]\geq-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|X_{1}|^{2}]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[|X_{2}|^{2}].

The result follows.
∎
